Can America Build More Housing Without Gutting Historic Protections? Inside the Senate’s Permitting Battle

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A sweeping permitting reform package moved closer to a final Senate vote Monday, when lawmakers voted 70–27 to limit debate on legislation that could weaken a federal safeguard for historic places.

At issue is Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act, which requires federal agencies to consider how projects they fund, approve, or permit might affect historic properties—and consult with those affected before proceeding.

The proposed changes would exempt most projects whose only federal connection is financial assistance, while imposing tighter deadlines and restricting legal challenges.

As the nation confronts a shortage of 4.03 million homes and an aging energy grid, supporters argue that reducing regulatory hurdles will accelerate infrastructure development. But preservation advocates warn the changes could leave communities with less influence over what gets built—and what gets lost.

"Preservation is not a hindrance to progress," Heather Veneziano, director of Tulane University's Historic Preservation Program, told Realtor.com® during a recent walking interview through Bayou St. John, one of New Orleans' oldest neighborhoods.

Rather than keeping neighborhoods frozen in time, she argued, preservation can help communities determine how they evolve.

"Keeping things static is impossible," Veneziano said.

New Orleans provides a particularly salient standpoint from which to understand Veneziano’s perspective.

The city is home to 26 nationally recognized historic districts encompassing more than 45,000 properties and roughly 40% of its urbanized area, according to the city. Indeed, its architectural heritage is so extensive that it became the only entire city included on the World Monuments Fund's Irreplaceable America list.

Veneziano helped lead the nomination, informed in part by the aftermath of Hurricane Katrina and the connections to place that could not be rebuilt elsewhere.

"You lose all your really strong ties and connections to place, but also to people," she said of the communities uprooted after the devastating 2005 superstorm. "And places don't matter without people."

But Veneziano sees the buildings themselves as part of what makes those communities viable.

"It's an economic driver for the community, it's housing at a basic level for the community," she said. "Those are the containers for all of the services that happen in your community."

Along Bayou St. John, Veneziano pointed to the Pitot House, built in 1799, as an example of another kind of value older buildings retain.

Its elevated living quarters reflect a time when residents built knowing water would eventually come, while its surviving materials offer lessons in constructing homes suited to Louisiana's flood risks.

Yet, those lessons might have been erased had preservationists not found a way to accommodate another pressing community need.

In 1964, the religious order that owned the property needed land for a girls' school. Rather than allow its demolition, preservationists raised money to move the house 220 feet down the street on flatbed trucks.

The compromise allowed both the school and the house to continue serving the community. And today, the deal serves as a reminder of how preservation can accommodate development rather than prevent it.

Not every historic property can be relocated, however, and the expense of finding alternatives can test even the most committed to protecting a neighborhood's housing resources.

In March, Jane Place Neighborhood Sustainability Initiative, a community land trust dedicated to permanently affordable housing, sought permission to demolish a century-old shotgun house in Mid-City.

The nonprofit argued that rehabilitation would be prohibitively expensive and higher-density construction would better serve the property.

Preservation authorities disagreed, finding insufficient evidence that the house was beyond repair. Jane Place eventually withdrew its appeal without a finalized replacement development plan.

The spat concerned local demolition rules, not Section 106. But it exposed competing interpretations of how best to protect a neighborhood's housing resources: Preserve an existing home or make room for more?

Veneziano acknowledged that unclear local requirements can frustrate homeowners trying to maintain or renovate their properties.

"If your municipality doesn't have their act together and isn't clear about what their guidelines are," she said, "it can be very challenging to homeowners."

In a city facing an estimated shortage of 44,000 affordable rental units, the case for preserving old buildings can be difficult to reconcile with the urgent need for new homes. But elsewhere in the Big Easy, a long-vacant public building offers another approach.

In September, a century-old firehouse reopened after decades of vacancy, transformed into seven affordable apartments above an early-childhood education center.

The mixed-use redevelopment cost approximately $8 million, including $1.35 million in state historic tax credits and $725,000 in federal disaster recovery funds.

"The Louisiana Avenue Firehouse shows us that demolition of historic buildings is not always the answer," Louisiana Housing Corporation Executive Director Kevin Delahoussaye said.

But the very public investment that helped save the building also triggered Section 106 review—and that additional requirement is what housing advocates argue can make federally assisted developments more expensive and difficult to finance.

Their argument is simple: More reviews take more time. More time costs more money. And combined, that can make a project not worth it.

YIMBY Action, which supports the proposed Section 106 exemption, estimates that at least $10 billion annually in federal housing and community development funding could move faster under the broader permitting package, citing delays that can jeopardize financing or discourage developers from accepting public money altogether.

Veneziano emphasized that preservationists aren't simply trying to "save this fancy house." Their work, she explained, is "really community engagement"—helping residents recognize and protect histories that might otherwise be overlooked.

That concern brought hundreds of preservationists, civil rights advocates, and tribal representatives to the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9 to oppose changes to Section 106.

"Our sacred places are living places of prayer," Harry Antonio Jr., governor of the Pueblo of Laguna in New Mexico, told the crowd.

The Senate proposal would narrow consideration of certain effects on historic places, including noise and visual impacts, raising concerns about the protection of sacred landscapes, burial grounds, and other culturally significant sites.

Veneziano pointed to a conflict farther upriver in Wallace, LA, where federal scrutiny exposed problems with a developer's assessment of a proposed grain terminal.

There, descendants of enslaved residents opposed the project, which would have been built near historic plantations, burial grounds, and their predominantly Black community.

A ProPublica investigation revealed that an archaeological consultant's original findings about potential harm to historic sites had been stripped from a report prepared for the developer. The Army Corps of Engineers subsequently rejected the assessment as insufficient and demanded further investigation.

In April 2024, the Corps determined that noise, light pollution, and the terminal's industrial appearance would adversely affect Wallace's historic character—precisely the kinds of impacts the Senate proposal would subject to a narrower standard of review.

The company behind the terminal abandoned the project later that year, blaming prolonged permitting delays. Residents celebrated the outcome as a victory.

Such lengthy reviews, however, may be the exception rather than the rule.

An October analysis by Virginia's Department of Historic Resources found that 85% of nearly 9,900 projects completed Section 106 review within 30 days. Just 6% took longer than 90 days, primarily because they required additional surveys or involved potential harm to historic properties.

Those figures aren't necessarily representative nationwide, nor do they establish whether reviews actually delayed construction.

"If you're trying to get a project through, yeah, it's annoying," Veneziano acknowledged.

Still, Section 106 doesn't prohibit development. It requires agencies to consider the consequences and potential alternatives before proceeding.

And as Congress weighs how to accelerate construction, lawmakers will also confront a more fundamental question: Who gets to determine what a community can afford to lose?

Veneziano posed a question of her own: "How do we take the best of what we have, and build upon it into the future?"

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

Quorum Cyber Completes Acquisition of Ontinue, Advancing Its Vision for Microsoft-First Autonomous Cyber Defense

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Combination Brings Together Agentic AI, Experienced Cyber Defenders and Deep Microsoft Expertise to Deliver Proactive, Continuous and Trusted Cyber Defense

EDINBURGH and ZURICH, Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Quorum Cyber, an award-winning Microsoft-centric cybersecurity services provider, today announced that it has completed its acquisition of

Ontinue, a leading provider of AI-powered managed security operations. The transaction brings together two Microsoft-focused cybersecurity companies with complementary capabilities and a shared ambition to help organizations stay ahead of cyber risk.

Quorum Cyber and Ontinue will continue to operate under their existing brands as the integration begins. The focus is to create a broader portfolio and a more complete approach to cyber resilience, while maintaining the trusted service and security outcomes customers rely on today.

Together, the organizations bring deep expertise across managed detection and response, proactive risk reduction, incident response, professional services, and AI-powered security operations. Their shared vision is to move cyber defense beyond responding to threats and toward a model that is proactive, autonomous, trusted and continuously improving.

Looking ahead, Quorum Cyber and Ontinue plan to unveil a unified portfolio in early 2027, bringing together the strengths of both organizations to deliver a broader, more proactive approach to cyber resilience.

For customers, the priority is continuity today and greater value over time. Customers will continue to receive the service and security outcomes they rely on, while gaining access to the broader expertise, innovation and services of the combined organization.

"This is a defining milestone for Quorum Cyber and Ontinue, but more importantly, it marks the beginning of what we build together," said Kevin Hanes, CEO of Quorum Cyber. "While this transaction is an acquisition, our mindset is that of a merger. Both companies have built strong businesses, teams, and customer relationships. Together, we have an opportunity to shape the future of cyber defense, combining agentic AI, experienced defenders and deep Microsoft expertise to keep customers ahead of cyber risk."

Advancing Proactive, Autonomous Cyber Defense

The combination reflects a shared belief that the future of cybersecurity will require more than faster detection and response. As AI accelerates both the speed of attacks and the pace of defense, organizations need a security model that can continuously identify risk, act with greater autonomy and improve over time without sacrificing human judgment, trust or accountability.

Ontinue's Agentic SOC and AI-powered managed security operations complement Quorum Cyber's cyber resilience, threat intelligence, incident response and professional services. These capabilities underpin the companies' mission to deliver a more complete approach to managing and reducing cyber risk.

"Ontinue was built on the belief that security operations needed to fundamentally change for the autonomous AI era, moving beyond alert detection and response to focus more broadly on reducing cyber risk and strengthening resilience," said Moritz Mann, CEO of Ontinue. "Joining Quorum Cyber gives us the opportunity to take that vision much further. We share a Microsoft-first foundation and a belief that agentic AI defends at machine speed with increasing agency and trust while humans make critical judgements and decisions. For our customers, this means preserving what they value today while expanding the capabilities and expertise available to them. For our people, it is an opportunity to apply their expertise to a broader mission and shape how AI-powered cyber defense evolves."

A Shared Commitment to Microsoft Security

The combination brings together two organizations with extensive experience across the Microsoft Security ecosystem.

Quorum Cyber is a Microsoft Solutions Partner for Security, holds all Microsoft Security specializations, is a member of the Microsoft Intelligent Security Association (MISA) and was named the 2025 Microsoft Security MSSP of the Year. Ontinue is also a MISA member and has been repeatedly recognized by Microsoft for its security innovation, including as Microsoft Security Services Innovator of the Year and through multiple Microsoft award finalist recognitions.

Together, the companies will draw on their Microsoft expertise to help customers get greater value from Microsoft Security technologies and evolve their security operations for an era in which both threats and defenses are increasingly powered by AI.

The closing follows Quorum Cyber's September 16, 2026 announcement that it had signed a definitive agreement to acquire Ontinue.

Eterna Growth Partners, a private investment firm and current majority investor of Quorum Cyber is the majority investor of the combined company. Quorum Cyber was advised by Mintz, Macfarlanes, and Schellenberg Wittmer and Ontinue was advised by Raymond James and BÃ¤r & Karrer.

Financial terms of the transaction were not disclosed.

Founded in Edinburgh in 2016, Quorum Cyber is a proactive, threat-led cybersecurity company helping organizations build resilience in an increasingly complex, AI-enabled threat landscape. With customers across North America, the UK and beyond, Quorum Cyber is a Microsoft Solutions Partner for Security, a member of the Microsoft Intelligent Security Association (MISA) and the 2025 Microsoft Security MSSP of the Year. For more information, visit www.quorumcyber.com.

Eterna Growth Partners ("Eterna") is a North American private equity firm specializing in middle-market technology investments. Built on the belief that enduring value comes from enduring partnership, Eterna employs a thematic investment approach and deep sector expertise to support management teams in accelerating growth, fostering innovation and building durable businesses. Eterna invests across vertical solutions, infrastructure technology, cybersecurity, healthcare IT, cloud solutions, and financial technology. Additional information is available at www.eternagrowth.com and LinkedIn.

A few things come to mind when I think about what the greatest fear of a Battlefield player is. It could be a glint atop the mountains on Liberation Peak, reliving the launch of Battlefield 2042, or maybe it's the randomised recoil from the first Battlefield. All of these are good bets, but I think the real shared nightmare of most Battlefield players is that someday it'll morph into Call of Duty.

To be fair, this fear isn't entirely unfounded. Bryan Beede was previously appointed as Battlefield's general manager after spending 19 years working on Call of Duty and has now been promoted to group general manager at EA. Taking his place is another Call of Duty veteran, Sam Cooper, who worked as Call of Duty's global product management and head of live service.

"I'm excited to share that I've joined Electronic Arts as general manager of Battlefield," Cooper says in a LinkedIn post. "I started my games career at EA on the FIFA team, so coming back to where it all began feels extra special.

"Battlefield is an iconic franchise that has something no other shooter has: all-out warfare, destruction at massive scale, squads play, and a passionate community that has stuck with this franchise for more than two decades."

Now this in and of itself isn't enough to sound any alarm bells for players concerned whether or not Battlefield can stay true to form, especially since Beede was appointed as Battlefield GM back in 2021 prior to Battlefield 6's release. But this alongside some questionable new skins has some Battlefield players worried that Battlefield is becoming more "en-cod-ified".

A big talking point during Battlefield 6's launch was players begging the devs to ignore COD's trend of gimmicky skins and instead stay true to its in-game realism for cosmetics. This has largely been the case—although the Dead Space collab was a near miss. But now some believe the cracks are starting to show with the new Fox Hunter skin pack.

"There's absolutely no way you can defend this," one player says in response to the skin pack. "There's a pseudo furry skin in Battlefield now. The defense I've heard is that 'it's plausible', while these are plausible, the masks are just, no? What tactical purpose does wearing a Japanese baby mask [or] a furry mask serve?" I'd argue, however, that it could stun an enemy Battlefield player long enough for you to secure a kill, and therefore there's at least some tactical purpose to it.

La Germania ha reso omaggio a Rudiger: 86 presenze in nazionale senza il grande trionfo

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Antonio Rudiger ha ricevuto l'ultimo applauso da calciatore della Nazionale tedesca. Il difensore, che aveva annunciato il ritiro dalla Germania dopo il Mondiale disputato negli Stati Uniti, è stato omaggiato all'Olympiastadion di Berlino davanti a oltre 70 mila spettatori prima della sfida di Nations League contro l'Olanda.

Dopo 86 presenze e dieci anni di avventura con la Mannschaft, il centrale classe 1993 ha chiuso un capitolo importante della sua carriera. Un percorso caratterizzato da grande continuità, ma anche dal rimpianto di non aver mai conquistato un grande trofeo con la maglia della Nazionale.

Il debutto di Rüdiger risale al 13 maggio 2014, nel pareggio senza reti contro la Polonia sotto la guida di Joachim Low. Poche settimane dopo arrivò il Mondiale vinto in Brasile, ma il difensore non fece parte della spedizione tedesca. Da quel momento è stato invece protagonista delle successive spedizioni, tutte terminate in modo deludente: presente ai Mondiali del 2018 in Russia, del 2022 in Qatar e all'ultima edizione disputata negli Stati Uniti, oltre agli Europei del 2021 e a quello casalingo del 2024. L'unico successo internazionale con la selezione maggiore resta la Confederations Cup conquistata nel 2017.

La carriera nei club ha avuto tutt'altra dimensione. Dopo le esperienze con Stoccarda, Roma e Chelsea, Rüdiger è diventato un pilastro del Real Madrid, con cui ha conquistato due Champions League. Il suo carattere e alcuni episodi dentro e fuori dal campo hanno spesso generato discussioni, ma nessuno ha mai messo in dubbio personalità e leadership. A Berlino, nella sua città natale, è arrivato il saluto definitivo della Germania al "ragazzo del Bollplatz". Un addio importante per un difensore che lascia la Nazionale senza il trionfo sognato, ma con un ruolo comunque centrale nella storia recente della Mannschaft.

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO, despite a growing campaign for his return led by some of the biggest names in tech and backed by President Donald Trump.

The board is focused on its investigation into Huang and is not in discussions to reinstate him, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Monday. The independent investigation, led by law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, is examining Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after 33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded. His departure followed his admission of an affair and reports that he had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at an Nvidia customer whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, as well as with Nvidia employees. The week of revelations saw hundreds of billions of dollars wiped from Nvidia's market value.

The report cuts against a weekend of growing pressure for Huang's return. The Financial Times reported that Huang had discussed a comeback with allies, though Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner, one of the investors urging the board to keep "all options" open, told CNBC that Huang had not discussed returning with him. A representative for Huang declined to comment on the speculation.

An online petition calling for Huang's reinstatement had drawn 191,996 signatures by Monday. Its backers include Elon Musk, who said on Friday that he was signing it, as well as Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. The site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. Nvidia employees have also pushed internally for his return.

Trump said on Monday that he would welcome Huang back. "I would certainly not mind seeing that," he told reporters, adding that national security had not been put at risk, as Decrypt reported. That puts him at odds with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO.

Others in tech have stopped short of backing a return. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, who was briefly ousted from his own company in 2023, said at a Semafor conference on Monday that Huang had "long been a hero of mine," but that he did not know enough to say whether Huang should come back.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.1% at $156.14 on Monday, and were down a further 0.5% at $155.67 in premarket trading early on Tuesday, according to Yahoo! Finance data.

Nvidia's board said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the CEO role. Chief financial officer Colette Kress, the interim CEO, will lead the company's quarterly earnings call on Tuesday.

Meta says it tuned Instagram to show less explicit content in India

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The change limits sexually suggestive and violent posts from accounts users don’t follow, after a government advisory in October.

Mark Zuckerberg on stage at Meta Connect 2026

Meta has changed how Instagram picks posts to show users in India. The aim is to show less sexually suggestive and violent content. The company made the change public on Tuesday, Reuters reported.

Meta now uses better AI models to check posts against its rules, Bloomberg reported. TNW has not seen Meta’s own statement and relies on both reports.

The change covers posts from accounts that users do not follow, according to Meta. It limits what Instagram pushes to users. It does not ban the posts.

Meta said users in India now flag suggested posts about half as often as a year ago. It did not share the raw figures, Reuters noted.

Users can also ask for fewer political posts and less sensitive content. In India, the most mature posts are not suggested by default, according to Bloomberg.

The update follows an October 10 notice from India’s tech ministry. It asked Meta to review its recommendation systems, so false or misleading posts do not spread too far.

Meta has faced months of pressure in India, its biggest market by users. In July, the ministry summoned Meta executives after the BBC found Instagram ads that pushed child abuse material. It then ordered the ads removed.

Instagram’s design is also being probed in Europe. In July, the EU told Meta to change Facebook and Instagram, such as autoplay and endless scroll.

Kehrer strikes in stoppage time as Bayer Leverkusen edge Stuttgart 3-2

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

Bayer Leverkusen beat Stuttgart 3-2 in the Frauen Bundesliga on Monday 16 November, with Caroline Victoria Kehrer scoring the winner in the second minute of stoppage time. Stuttgart had just drawn level through Mira Arouna in the 87th minute. Julia Boike refereed the matchday 10 fixture.

Leverkusen led at the break. Loreen Bender opened the scoring in the 20th minute, set up by Cornelia Kramer, and the visitors took a 1-0 lead into half-time.

Stuttgart answered immediately after the restart. Dafina Redzepi equalised in the 46th minute, and the Stuttgart forward went on to have the biggest influence on the match. She was rated 8.86, the highest on the pitch, with a goal, an assist, 3 shots and 5 key passes across the full 90 minutes.

Leverkusen restored their advantage in the 59th minute when Maja Sternad finished from a Valentina Mädl pass. Sternad was booked 3 minutes later, after Luzie Zähringer had been cautioned for Stuttgart in the 54th minute. Those were the only cards of the night.

Redzepi then turned provider, laying on the 87th-minute equaliser for Arouna, who scored with her only shot in 21 minutes on the pitch. The draw lasted barely 5 minutes. Mädl delivered again, and Kehrer converted in the second minute of stoppage time, having been on the field for just 2 minutes. Mädl finished with 2 assists in a 33-minute appearance.

The numbers favoured the winners without being one-sided. Leverkusen had 54% of the ball, completed 366 of 483 passes against Stuttgart’s 266 of 387, and put 8 shots on target. Stuttgart goalkeeper L. Giuliani’s side were kept in the game by 6 saves. Stuttgart actually shot more often, 15 times to 13, but only 4 of those efforts tested the Leverkusen goal and 8 went wide. Expected goals finished 1.43 to 1.2 in Leverkusen’s favour.

Leverkusen were caught offside 4 times. Stuttgart were not flagged once.

The win leaves Leverkusen fifth on 19 points, level with Werder Bremen in fourth and separated only by goal difference, 4 against 6. Eintracht Frankfurt are third on 20 points. Bayern Munich lead on 30 points from 10 games, with VfL Wolfsburg second on 24.

It is a second straight league win for Leverkusen after a 2-0 victory over Hamburger SV. Kramer, provider for the opener, has 6 league goals this season, 2 behind leading scorer E. Peddemors of VfL Wolfsburg.

For Stuttgart, the defeat ended a two-match winning run that had brought a 3-0 win over SGS Essen and a 3-1 victory against Mainz 05. Before that, they had lost three in a row, conceding 12 goals against Union Berlin, Bayern Munich and Eintracht Frankfurt. They are ninth with 11 points and a goal difference of -11.

Prediction: The Stock Market Will Make a Big Move on Nov. 18 (Hint: Nvidia Will Be the Catalyst)

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia's third-quarter earnings report could send shockwaves through the stock market.

Nvidia (NVDA -4.12%) has been one of the market's most influential companies during the artificial intelligence boom, but the stock has struggled in 2026. Shares have declined 16% year to date, while the S&P 500 (^GSPC -1.92%) has added 8% and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -2.49%) has added 5%.

Investor uncertainty intensified last week when Nvidia announced the sudden resignation of CEO Jensen Huang. The company will announce its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results after the market closes on Tuesday, Nov. 17. Those results could trigger substantial volatility across the stock market the next day.

Nvidia's revenue growth has accelerated in five consecutive quarters amid strong demand for artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure, and the company beat Wall Street's consensus earnings estimate in each of those quarters. But the bar is especially high for the upcoming report, given growing skepticism about the durability of the AI boom.

Here's what Wall Street expects when Nvidia announces its financial results for the third quarter of fiscal 2027 after the market closes on Tuesday, Nov. 17:

Beyond the headline numbers, investors should pay attention to management's guidance for the fourth quarter. Nvidia's outlook will provide important clues about demand for AI infrastructure, particularly as technology companies continue to invest enormous sums of money in data centers.

Investors should also listen for commentary concerning the next-generation Vera Rubin compute platform (which started shipping in the last few months), competition from custom AI chips, and whether Nvidia customers are generating sufficient returns to justify their capital expenditures.

Finally, this will be the first earnings call since former CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang resigned. Interim CEO Colette Kress will likely face questions about the board's search for a permanent successor and whether the leadership change will disrupt Nvidia's technology roadmap or relationship with major suppliers and customers.

Nvidia is one of the largest companies in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite, meaning the stock has a disproportionate impact on the performance of both indexes. Nvidia's earnings often influence market sentiment toward other semiconductor companies and the broader AI industry.

With that in mind, the chart below details how Nvidia performed on the day following its last four quarterly earnings announcements. It also shows how the S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite performed on those days. Importantly, Nvidia beat consensus revenue and earnings estimates in each of the four quarters listed below.

Data source: YCharts. The chart shows how Nvidia shares and the broader stock market performed on the day following Nvidia's last four earnings reports.

As shown above, Nvidia stock usually declined on the day following its last four quarterly earnings reports, dropping by an average of 0.5%. Meanwhile, the broader S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite also typically suffered losses on those days.

This time, strong results and optimistic guidance could reassure Wall Street that massive investments in AI infrastructure remain sustainable, potentially sparking a rally across the semiconductor industry and technology sector, or even the broader stock market. Having said that, history makes it clear that solid financial results do not necessarily translate into share price appreciation.

Conversely, disappointing guidance or cautious commentary about customer spending could increase anxiety about an AI bubble, especially given that Jensen Huang recently left the company. That could easily lead to a sell-off, and the drawdown could be severe. That's why I think Nov. 18 will be an unusually consequential day for the stock market.

Carney and Xi Announce Canada-China Trade Pact

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The agreement between Canada and China and the cordial relations on display by their leaders contrast with the current Canada-U.S. relationship.

Prime Minister Mark Carney of Canada and China’s leader, Xi Jinping, have struck an agreement to reduce some tariffs between their countries, a largely symbolic move that shows relations between the two countries are warming as Canada’s ties to the United States falter.

While the expanded trade between China and Canada resulting from the accord in the Chinese tech and financial hub Shenzhen will be modest, involving just $280 million in exports, the pact — as well as the seemingly amicable meeting — illustrates a contrast to Mr. Carney’s relationship with President Trump. Earlier this year, Mr. Carney said that the United States was “at war” with Canada after trade negotiations between the two countries collapsed in acrimony.

Talks are restarting between Canada and the United States but there is no indication that Washington will back away from key irritants including tariffs of up to 50 percent on Canadian autos, steel and aluminum.

Since entering politics last year, Mr. Carney has been traveling the world to diversify Canada’s economic and political ties away from the United States. Those efforts include current talks to make Canada the European Union’s first associate member and Mr. Carney’s second formal meeting this year with Mr. Xi.

“The stabilization of our bilateral trade arrangement is indicative of how we can work together more broadly, pragmatically, to solve problems,” Mr. Carney said at the start of the meeting with Mr. Xi on Tuesday.

He added that Canada is ahead of schedule with its goal of a 50 percent increase in exports to China by the end of the decade.

Sony has issued another system software update for PS5, and if you want access to any online features, you'll be forced to download and install it.

Yes, this is a required update; it brings the firmware to version 26.07-14.23.00, and is a fairly small 1.3 GB.

What does it do, then? Well, as was the case with the last update, it has one solitary patch note:

The folks at PlayStation have been really cracking down hard on the console's security. This update comes about two weeks after the last, which was also about a fortnight removed from the previous one — an unusually speedy deployment.

This is for a couple of reasons. The first is that hackers have been jailbreaking the PS5 in new and faster ways, utilising generative AI tech to find workarounds and weaknesses in the console's software infrastructure.

Secondly, GTA 6 is on the verge of release.

This is pertinent because, well, it's GTA 6; it's very possible it'll be the biggest game launch of all time. In other words, there's a heightened risk that people will want to hack their PS5 and gain access to the game without paying.

Sony has been making it so that your console must have the latest firmware installed to even boot up GTA 6, and it's highly likely today's update will indeed be a necessity if you want to play the game. This theoretically minimises the risk of jailbreaking and piracy.

With the game now less than 48 hours away, Sony has sent out one more pre-launch security measure in its battle against hackers.

Singapore defends status as a ‘trusted business hub’ after the U.S. proposes extending AI chip controls to Southeast Asia

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The U.S. proposed tighter export controls on AI hardware on Monday (Nov. 16), mandating licenses for firms looking to ship advanced chips to four Southeast Asian nations: Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand and Indonesia.

The rule change comes days after reports of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s ties to Megaspeed, a cloud computing firm which allegedly funneled over $7.5 billion of Nvidia chip shipments to Chinese firms via its regional subsidiaries. (Megaspeed says it is cooperating with U.S. inspections and denies wrongdoing)

The new rule also requires large data centers anywhere in the world to get a license if it wants to offer computing power to companies based in China.

Singapore, where Megaspeed is headquartered, is now trying to defend its reputation.

“Singapore is a trusted international business hub, underpinned by a strong rule of law,” a government spokesperson wrote in a press statement dated Nov. 16. “We take our international reputation seriously and do not condone companies using their association with Singapore to circumvent or violate the laws of other countries.”

The country added that it is engaging U.S. authorities to review details of the new measure, while working with affected companies to understand the potential impact on their businesses.

Thailand Vice Minister for Commerce, Kirida Bhaopichitr, told CNBC that they were ‘prepared’ for the new rule. “We don’t want to be a hub for transshipment of these goods from the U.S. to China, so we’ll more closely scrutinize these companies, especially data centers.”

Indonesia, too, said it was reviewing the rule, and “seeking industry input on its potential impact on operations and investment, including in the data centre sector”, according to an AFP report.

Malaysia and Indonesia have worked to step up efforts to curb chip smuggling. As part of an inquiry requested by the U.S. FBI to trace Nvidia chips bought by Megaspeed, Indonesian government officials raided the office of PT Megaspeed International Indonesia, the Indonesian subsidiary of Huang’s firm Megaspeed, earlier this year.

According to a Nov. 16 report by Singapore newspaper The Straits Times, Indonesian officials who inspected the premises found unused server racks at the site with missing graphics processing units (GPUs), which suggests that the chips were never installed there.

On Nov. 13, Malaysia’s Ministry of Investment, Trade and Industry (MITI) said it was carrying out checks with local customs officials to “determine whether any of the chips brought into Malaysia have since been re-exported”, and if so, if they were “in accordance with the law”.

Data center operators in Southeast Asia are also trying to figure out what the new rule means for their business.

“It’s still an interim rule,” Serene Nah, the APAC head of American data center provider Digital Realty, said at a roundtable at the launch of the Digital Realty Innovation Lab in Singapore on Nov. 17, the day after the measure was announced. “There might be ongoing items pertaining to registrations, licenses and things like that, but details are not out yet.”

The data center provider currently operates three facilities in Singapore, and entered Indonesia last March through a joint venture with Bersama Digital Infrastructure Asia, a Singapore-incorporated digital infrastructure firm. In June, it opened a campus in Cyberjaya, Malaysia, which is designed to support approximately 32 MW of data center capacity for hybrid cloud and AI workloads.

The firm’s executives remain positive that the U.S.’s new export rule won’t dampen the demand for AI compute in Southeast Asia, especially due to data sovereignty concerns among businesses and governments in the region.

“AI is pervasive,” said Chris Sharp, Digital Realty’s chief technology officer, at the Singapore facility launch. He pointed to financial services and healthcare players as their key customers in the region. “They want the full benefit of AI capabilities with zero exposure of their infrastructure publicly. They don’t want any data being routed over the internet.”

Global hyperscalers like AWS, Google and Microsoft, too, will likely continue looking to Southeast Asia to build out their data centers, given its abundance of cheap land and electricity.

“We certainly have and will see more enterprise customers wanting to deploy across Southeast Asia,” Nah concluded, adding that firms may keep their Asia headquarters in Singapore and their data center operations in another Southeast Asian country.

Angelica Ang is a Singapore-based journalist who covers the Asia-Pacific region.

2 Great Space Stocks Team Up on a $1.3 Billion Deal to Beat SpaceX at Satellite Internet

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AST SpaceMobile will pay Firefly $1.3 billion for some new rocket engines -- but how much of that cash will Firefly actually ever receive?

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -1.26%) dominates America's space sector. Starting off as a rocket launcher for other companies, SpaceX has evolved into a company that makes most of its money by using its own satellites to provide communications services on Earth -- a task made easier by having its own fleet of reusable rockets.

Other space companies aren't so fortunate.

AST SpaceMobile (ASTS -12.33%), for example, invented and was first to prove the concept of using satellites to connect calls between two ordinary, off-the-shelf cellphones (not specialized satellite phones). Lacking its own rockets, however, AST has to date been dependent upon other companies to provide the launch services it needs to get its satellites into orbit.

Which launch providers in particular? So far, AST has flown its satellites with India's ISRO, Blue Origin, and SpaceX itself. That last one is obviously suboptimal, though -- it's not a great corporate strategy to make yourself dependent on your biggest competitor for launch services!

And there are other options. The list of private companies that have successfully flown a rocket to orbit may not be a long one, but it's getting longer:

A couple of other names might deserve to be on this list, having flown once or twice but not continually. But for the most part, this is it. And one of these launch names jumps out especially: Firefly.

Last week, you see, Firefly offered to help AST out of its jam ... and collect a big payday for the service. For $1.3 billion, Firefly agreed to sell AST SpaceMobile 36 rocket engines, which is enough to outfit three full-size heavy lift rockets -- which AST intends to build itself. To this end, Firefly will provide AST engineering support over three years as AST builds a fleet of 20-ton "Blacklift" rockets (which it expects to have ready to fly by late 2028).

Firefly will also grant AST a license to use Firefly designs, enabling AST to build additional engines for additional rockets on its own.

For Firefly, the advantages of this deal seem obvious. Firefly gets $1.3 billion to build and deliver 36 engines (so about $36 million per engine). Firefly can then reinvest this money in itself "to rapidly expand our rocket production."

Viewed from the perspective of a Firefly investor, the only quibble I have with this deal is the form of payment. Firefly says AST will pay the $1.3 billion in "cash and shares of AST Class A Common Stock." The cash part is fine. What worries me is that Firefly might accept too much of its payment in the form of ASTS stock.

I'm not at all convinced that AST SpaceMobile can build and fly a rocket successfully by 2028.

As veteran space journalist Eric Berger at Ars Technica has pointed out, this timeline isn't just optimistic; it's "wildly, fantastically, and stupidly optimistic." After all, the fastest record for taking a rocket from design to orbital flight (ever) was set by SpaceX with the Falcon 9 -- and that took 4.7 years, more than twice the time AST is budgeting for.

While you can't rule out the possibility that AST will accomplish the feat, I wouldn't bet on it.

Nor would I invest in AST stock based on its promise to do so. Nor -- and this part is the most important for Firefly investors -- would I allow AST to pay me for my engines in too much ASTS stock.

So how much ASTS stock is Firefly accepting in payment for its engines?

Well, of the $1.3 billion headline number, only the first $400 million will be paid up front in cash, with an additional $400 million paid in ASTS stock. A tranche of $250 million will be paid upon delivery of the engines, with $250 million owed for engineering services. When all's said and done, as much as $900 million of the $1.3 billion may be paid in hard currency. But at least $400 million -- and potentially more -- could end up paid in ASTS stock, which could turn out to be worthless if (read "when") it fails to launch Blacklift on schedule.

TL;DR: This deal may not be as good news for Firefly investors as it appears on the surface, and Firefly may end up with a whole lot less cash than investors expect.

james bridle and the power of solar, zooplankton, and non-human entities

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ancient symbols and microscopic organisms meet contemporary solar technology in an artistic reflection on energy, ecology, and coexistence.

annalise kamegawa November 17, 2026 7 min

Back in 2022, James Bridle—artist, technologist, and author of the book New Dark Age (2018)—created a work called Solar Panels (Radiolaria Series). It was a series of five functional 100W photovoltaic generators, the faces of which were etched with tiny, microscopic zooplankton called Radiolaria. Looking at images of their silica skeletons, enlarged hundreds of times so they are visible to the human eye, reveals a universe of spiky arms, swirling bodies, intricate lattices, and geometric patterns. Juxtaposed with each other, and containing their own advanced modes of capturing energy, Bridle creates a compelling parallel between non-human and human intelligence, and what that could mean for the future of the earth.

Across their writing, installations, and lectures, James Bridle has become known for thinking about the ways that technology, the natural world, and humanity influence each other, with a strong emphasis on understanding ‘intelligence’. They look beyond the popular, mainstream ideas associated with the word, often linked to IQ, large language models, and other forms of reconstructing human thought patterns. In their piece, AI was built on human intelligence. Nature has more to teach, they express the urgency of going beyond this definition, and to learn from the other creatures we share the Earth with.

It is this line of thinking that has underwritten their works with solar panels, water collection systems, and vernacular architecture. Looking back to their 2019 exhibition, Through Other Eyes, which they curated with NeMe Art Centre in Cyprus, an emphasis on more-than-human forms of thinking and creating is evident. Here, they invoked the 1922 writing of Dziga Vertov, in which he describes his fascination with documentation through the lens of a camera. Bridle parallels this with the technologies of the twenty-first century – all the mania, fascination, and consequences – saying that, if we apply these new or different ‘eyes’, those of machines and animals, ‘They might also, if we are open to them, offer us visions of unexpected insight, and startling beauty.’ It is within this vision – trans-species and trans-machine – that Bridle’s work takes form.

Things Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, by James Bridle at Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell AnyoneThings Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, by James Bridle at Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell Anyone

Putting an image of radiolaria on a solar panel conjures ideas about the ability to sustain life and other modalities of using energy generated by natural processes. It calls into question the human need to consume power. Radiolaria, microscopic yet possessing highly advanced skeletal structures, can exist symbiotically with algae that provide them with energy. There is an ancient logic to their existence that can be traced back to the Cambrian. Bridle, in their description of the project, highlights how they were ‘famously illustrated, by the German naturalist Ernst Haeckel, who coined the term “ecology” in 1866.’ The complex structures of these organisms continue to inspire wonder, demonstrating how something so small can be so powerful and complex.

Expanding this logic to the scale of solar panels, a technologically intensive mode of capturing and converting energy from the sun in a way that can power human inventions calls into question the effectiveness of human ‘technology’ when compared to that of the non-human. Here is an incredible invention that can create sustainable energy, a promising alternative to fossil fuels, yet it takes advanced electronics and logistics to achieve what a single-celled organism has been doing for thousands of years. Here, Bridle once again asks us to consider what innovation means, not when we compare it to the span of human history.

In 2023, Bridle expanded on the concept in Things Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, an installation on the archaeological site of Elefsina in Greece, Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell Anyone, just north of Athens. Instead of radiolaria, the images engraved on three 100W solar photovoltaic panels are ‘the wheatsheaf, the poppy, and the calathus,’ which ‘honor the ancient cult of Demeter, and the veneration of the living Earth which she represents.’ Here, the comparison of contemporary power generation with beliefs in ancient deities asks us to question the ways in which energy is created, used, and distributed. Bridle used solar panels to power other works in the installation and the site itself, going on to remark that ‘The technology to do these things, is available to us, and the knowledge of how to do them is not a mystery, nor a secret, but it might require new rituals, and new initiations, to distribute it more widely. This is one of those rituals.’

the poppy that would be inscribed on Bridle’s solar panelssketch of Things Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, an installation on the archaeological site of Elefsina in Greece, Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell Anyone by James Bridle

Ultimately, James Bridle’s solar panel creations reflect a broader vision of posthuman utopias that runs throughout their artistic practice: one in which technology, humanity, and the natural world exist in a more reciprocal relationship. By bringing together ancient natural intelligence and contemporary technologies, Bridle challenges human exceptionalism and conventional ideas of progress. Their work encourages us to look beyond ourselves for models of innovation, recognizing that the natural world already holds sophisticated forms of knowledge. In doing so, they invite us to imagine futures founded on ecological interdependence, collaboration, and coexistence.

Things Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, by James Bridle at Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell AnyoneThings Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, by James Bridle at Mystery 19 Visual Arts Initiator III – But Don’t Tell Anyone | image by Vangelis Gkinis

works: Solar Panels (Radiolaria Series), 2022 & Things Said, Things Seen, and Things Done, 2023

Appian Advances Control Plane Capabilities To Drive Safety and Business Value from AI Agents

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New capabilities let organisations track, orchestrate, and optimise AI agents inside deterministic business processes

LONDON, Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Appian (Nasdaq: APPN) shared new agent management capabilities at Appian Europe, its annual conference in London. With foundational capabilities available now and additional features planned in upcoming releases, the Appian Platform provides a control plane to ensure the work agents do creates reliable business value.  Appian also announced an agentic interface that simplifies how work gets done.

Appian's control plane orchestrates agents inside deterministic business processes, monitors their activities, and ensures that agents create business value. "Concerns about agent safety are driving demand for control planes to enforce permissions, guardrails, and model access," said Sanat Joshi, Executive Vice President of Product Management at Appian. "Those matter, but they can't tell you whether an agent is doing useful work. Appian is different. We go beyond technical controls to focus on value. Appian puts business process at the center, so you can keep agents in bounds, see how each agent impacts business outcomes, and use agents to transform your operations."

Appian's control plane capabilities address a  growing concern as organisations scale AI adoption - value. A 2026 study conducted by Appian and Harvard Business Review Analytics Services found that just 16% of respondents reported a high degree of measurable value from AI.

Appian is a recognised Leader in the 2026 GartnerÂ® Magic Quadrantáµá´¹ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies (BOAT). As such, Appian starts with deterministic business processes and defines the agent's role in the work alongside other resources. The control plane covers three areas:

Raiffeisen Bank International (RBI), a leading corporate and retail bank in Central and Eastern Europe, intends to automate payment inquiries with the Appian platform. In testing, the team found that orchestrating and fine-tuning AI agents alongside rules-based automation significantly reduced processing time and token consumption.

"A large part of this reduction came from our Smart Automation team's process-centric approach to orchestration," said Claus Mitterlehner, Head of Smart Automation, Raiffeisen Bank International. "Our first instinct was to assign most tasks to AI agents mainly. After testing it, the team changed course and moved the predictable steps to deterministic automation, keeping agents for the steps that need judgment. The result is now more economical and more reliable, and their hybrid approach reduced token consumption and processing time by 80%."

Appian also unveiled Ask Appian, a new agentic interface that transforms how people interact with systems. Ask Appian helps eliminate the need for employees to navigate complex enterprise software or hunt across disconnected systems for information. Users simply describe the outcome they want in plain language and Ask Appian pulls the relevant context across systems connected through Appian's data fabric and dispatches agents to complete the work end to end.

Many control plane capabilities are available today, including controls, orchestration, and process intelligence. Additional capabilities will become available starting this year.

Customers can join Appian's early adopter program to use the control plane and access capabilities ahead of general availability.

Cassava reportedly seeks up to $1bn for Nvidia chips for African AI

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strive Masiyiwa’s Cassava is in talks with Middle Eastern and US investors to fund GPUs for its African data centres.

Strive Masiyiwa speaks alongside then Secretary of State Antony Blinken and then Assistant Secretary for African Affairs Molly Phee at the U.S.-Africa Leaders Summit foreign ministers’ dinner in Washington, 14 December 2022.

Cassava Technologies is seeking to raise as much as $1bn to buy Nvidia chips for its African AI data centres, Loni Prinsloo reported for Bloomberg. The report cites people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named.

Cassava declined to comment to Bloomberg, and TNW has not independently verified the report. The talks are ongoing and may not lead to a deal, the people said.

The company, founded by Zimbabwean billionaire Strive Masiyiwa, is talking to investors in the Middle East and the US, according to Bloomberg. Most of the money would buy graphics processing units (GPUs), the chips that train and run AI models.

Some would also prepare Cassava’s fibre and data centre networks for AI workloads, the people said. Its Cape Town facility, built to Nvidia’s specifications, is already running at full capacity, Bloomberg reported.

Cassava is expanding through partners. In Egypt, it is working with Vodafone Business and Elsewedy Electric on a $1bn data centre project that includes the country’s first sovereign AI data centre. Vodafone Egypt is part of Vodacom Group.

Rivals are moving too. MTN, Africa’s largest mobile operator, has set up a data centre venture with a UAE-based investor. It plans AI-ready sites in South Africa and Nigeria.

Africa has less than 1% of the world’s AI data centres, according to Bloomberg. Gulf investors may also see Africa as a way to spread risk, after data centres in the Gulf became targets during the conflict involving the US, Israel and Iran.

A successful raise would help Cassava compete with Microsoft, Amazon and Google, which are all investing in Africa.

Steven Gerrard says Arsenal star has given Mikel Arteta ‘a big problem’

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Steven Gerrard believes Ezri Konsa has already given Mikel Arteta ‘a big problem’ after joining Arsenal.

Konsa was signed by Arsenal in a £55 million deal from Aston Villa in August and has hit the ground running for the Premier League champions in the absence of William Saliba.

However, Saliba appears to be nearing a comeback from his back injury as he made the bench for Arsenal’s 2-1 win over Slavia Prague in the Champions League earlier this month.

Arteta now faces a tricky selection decision at centre-back when Saliba, Konsa and Gabriel Magalhaes are all available over the winter period.

‘I think Konsa has improved so much under Unai Emery,’ Gerrard said on The Overlap Daily.

‘I think he’s given Mikel Arteta a big problem, Konsa, the way he’s played for Arsenal.

Microsoft's SideWinder Strategic Commander was a failed attempt to reimagine RTS controls. 23 years later, why are some players still using it?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

How an unconventional peripheral became an equally unexpected survivor

PC players have been conditioned for decades to accept the keyboard-and-mouse setup for RTS games. One hand has to handle movement and commands through a keyboard, while the other points, drags, and selects with a mouse. Peripheral makers have, usually with the addition of more buttons, made many minor variations to this time-honored arrangement, but there's only ever been one real attempt to rethink it entirely for the PC: the Microsoft SideWinder Strategic Commander.

Unveiled on March 1st 2000 at Microsoft's Gamestock event, the Strategic Commander was one of three additions to their SideWinder line, with a scheduled release that October at a pricey $64.95. At E3 later that May, Microsoft demonstrated the Strategic Commander for the first time with SimCity 3000, while promoting its potential for other such games as Diablo II, Warcraft III, and Baldur's Gate II. This was all taking place during the so-called 'Golden Age of Real-Time Strategy Games,' which spanned roughly from the mid-1990s to the mid-2000s. It was when the RTS genre was at the peak of its mainstream popularity, and we fans were devoted to mastering its intricacies in any way we could to have a competitive edge.

Microsoft, all the while, were taking notes. "The team conceived the Strategic Commander in the late 1990s," Carl Ledbetter - then Industrial Design Manager for Microsoft's PC Hardware Group, now Head of Design for Xbox Devices - recalls to me. "As RTS games grew in popularity, we saw an interesting opportunity to design a new physical interface around a new interaction model. The idea was for it to be used left-handed in concert with right-hand PC desktop mouse control. We wanted to enable players to perform faster with more fluidity, comfort, optimised control placement for multiple hand sizes, simplicity of set-up, and ease of programming."

For Ledbetter and co. to realise this ambition, the Strategic Commander was designed as a unique combination of mouse, joystick, and gamepad. To manage the increasing complexities of the RTS games of the day, it came equipped with six programmable buttons, three shift buttons, and a three-position profile switch. Back then, these atypical features provided an unprecedented 72 possible key combinations across various configurations, all at the tip of our stubby little fingers. Better yet, it allowed us to toggle between three pre-configured profiles at a time, so that we could switch up our configurations on the fly depending on which stage of an RTS match we were in.

On release, contemporary reviewers acknowledged its originality, with critics like ActiveWin's Julien Jay describing it as "probably the most innovative (with the GameVoice) and revolutionary" peripheral designed specifically for strategy players. Others, like Neoseeker's Arnel Lim, suggested that it might’ve gone overboard with its customisation options, saying "you won't use anywhere close to all of them," in reference to the controller's programmable key combinations. Either way, the Strategic Commander failed to supplant conventional peripherals in the hands of most RTS players, and it was discontinued in 2003. As was the rest of the original SideWinder line, owing to declining sales and the industry-wide shift toward standard M+K setups for all PC games and genres.

However, unlike so many of its Microsoft-made siblings, this was not the end for the Strategic Commander. Niche as it was, it went on to enjoy a surprisingly long post-shelf life, one that was stretched out by a dedicated cult following - and that continues to this day. But why? Why, after all these years, is this forgotten peripheral from a discontinued line maintaining such a enthusiastic fanbase, years after the industry around it had written it off as a failure?

The answer lies in software and OS compatibility, as much as the hardware's own appeal. Andreas Weis was the developer of one of the first unofficial patches for the Strategic Commander, allowing players to keep on using it even after the original software stopped being compatible with newer versions the Windows. By that point, the device was a decade old, and well into 'official' obsolescence, yet Weis still answered the call of the Strategic Commander's righteously frustrated - if numerically few - fans. "There was a lot of interest back in the early 2010s in software patches to keep the Strategic Commander functional when the original software for the Strategic Commander stopped working," he explains. "Users were asking around on internet forums about how to get it to work on Windows 7 and Vista machines. Since I was studying computer science at the time, I was quite interested in reverse-engineering, and I hacked together a simple tool that would cover my use cases for the device."

In the years that followed, other patches were released to keep the Strategic Commander operational, the most notable of which was Philip Merwarth’s Strategic Engine software in 2013. Unfortunately, they weren’t open-source. Worse was that the protocol for programming the Strategic Commander wasn’t documented anywhere online in a way that was accessible for users. This is why Weis realized that he could make a unique contribution.

"I polished my old prototype code for the Strategic Commander software patch and released it as a library, so that other people could use it to write their own applications for programming the tool," he says. "As for me, I released one application that made use of the library publicly, the vJoy feeder application, which allows the device to be used with games that support joystick inputs." Curiously, Weis, by this time and like many others, had found other applications for a device with such a specific selling point, and even continues to use the Strategic Commander sparingly in 2026.

"The production quality of Microsoft hardware at the time was just top-notch, and I love the design of the old SideWinder devices, which is why I did not just throw the thing out when the software stopped working," he tells me. "It was worth keeping around to find new uses for it over the years, and I eventually realized that it makes quite a nice addition to a HOTAS (Hands-On Throttle And Stick) setup, where I could put actions that aren’t time-critical and don't fit well on the throttle on the Strategic Commander instead. For example, when I’m playing Elite Dangerous in VR, I’d put actions like deploying the landing gear on the Strategic Commander to free up buttons on the throttle for actions that were more relevant for combat."

When looking back at the history of Microsoft’s SideWinder line, it’s easy to call the Strategic Commander a failed experiment, given the fact that most of us are still using the same standard mouse-and-keyboard setup to play RTS games. However, the central premise behind its design philosophy was that the interface between a player and a game could be redesigned to cater to specific genre quirks, and decades following its release, it’s still maintained a coalition of loyal users: some RTS players who believed the Strategic Commander really pulled it off, and those found other uses for the device in other flavours of PC game. And with the issue of modern compatibility, a roadblock for so much retro hardware, bypassed by homemade patches, both camps can continue twisting and clicking their Strategic Commanders unimpeded.

All the same, I wonder if it might have basked in more conventional success, had Microsoft made more forward-thinking decisions. The Strategic Commander has proven over the years that its unprecedented customisation had applications far beyond what Ledbetter’s team had envisioned, and once the popularity of RTS games settled down, other, similar click-heavy subgenres like MOBAs and Real-Time Tactics got big as well. Would a more officially maintained, potentially refined Strategic Commander model have served those crowds? I think it might. The narrow focus on RTS games may have also hurt the device's since-demonstrated broader appeal; if Microsoft had fully capitalised on this customisation selling point by communicating its benefits to everyone, not just those playing the single most popular PC genre at the time, it could have provided the blueprint for a proper, long-lived alternative to controllers, mice, and keyboards.

Alas, gaming history is littered with revolutionary peripherals that failed to actually revolutionise the industry surrounding them. The Strategic Commander is no different, but for the fact that it’s had this surprisingly long post-shelf life - and, as a result, the opportunity for insight into what could have been done to salvage the its failures. Still, that unexpected longevity can provide come comfort to the Strategic Commander's creators, as well as its lingering users.

"I think the design made sense for its time, especially with the rising popularity of RTS games," Ledbetter reflects. "Looking back, I would have liked more time to test and refine the Strategic Commander. I’m proud of what we brought to market, and I’m glad to know that it remains beloved by many players to this day."

La Gorce Island Point Lot Overlooking Biscayne Bay Is Listed for an Eye-Watering $170 Million

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A spectacular waterfront mansion on Miami’s guard-gated La Gorce Island has hit the market for a jaw-dropping $170 million.

Built in 2015, the legacy estate is reportedly owned by Betsy Sherman, wife of George Sherman, the late former chairman of Campbell’s. If the property fetches anywhere near its asking price, it would rank among Miami’s most expensive home sales ever, according to The Wall Street Journal.

The Shermans purchased the property for just $17 million in 2006 and custom-built the sprawling 18,213-square-foot estate, which now claims the top spot on the Realtor.com® weekly list of the most expensive homes.

Boasting 320 feet of Biscayne Bay frontage, the residence was designed to capture spectacular sunset views from nearly every room.

Interior highlights include an expansive great room with sliding glass doors that open to the outdoor living area, a formal dining room, a modern kitchen, and a bar.

A sculptural, curved glass staircase leads to the sleeping quarters, where a lavish primary retreat features a private terrace overlooking the bay.

Additional amenities include a fitness center, bunk room, pool, four-car garage, and deep-water dock designed to accommodate yachts.

More spectacular mansions to make the week's most expensive homes list include a Utah mountain retreat under construction, an oceanfront estate in Hawaii with sweeping Pacific Ocean views, and a rebuilt Palm Beach, FL, sanctuary.

Gold Coast: This 50-foot-wide double lot on the north side of Broadway has panoramic views from San Francisco Bay and the Golden Gate Bridge to Alcatraz and the Marin Headlands.

Built in 1908, the seven-bedroom estate was reconstructed in 2007 and has 9,001 square feet of reimagined space. The four-level home has herringbone-patterned floors, arched doorways, built-in bookshelves, custom millwork, seven fireplaces, and an elevator to each floor. A stained-glass skylight illuminates the sculptural staircase.

The formal living room is surrounded by floor-to-ceiling windows framing the waterfront views. The professional-grade kitchen, with a butler’s pantry and breakfast bar, flows into the dining room, which has patio access. The lower level has a wine cellar, a secondary laundry room, service areas, and storage space.

A private sport court is surrounded by mature greenery.

Neal Ward of Compass is the listing agent.

Teardown opportunity: This 2.33-acre property offers a rare chance to “create a dream estate on a truly grand scale.”

Built in 1959, the existing nine-bedroom home has 16,290 square feet of space. Offered “with the freedom to build from the ground up,” the property is in the country’s most expensive ZIP code and near the tech hubs of Silicon Valley. No interior photos are included with the listing, but it does show a swimming pool.

The expansive lot has room for gardens and large-scale entertaining.

Ken Deleon of Deleon Realty is the listing agent.

Fjell House: This 11,632-square-foot mountain retreat will be the largest new home in the gated Stein Eriksen Estates in Deer Valley when it is completed in 2027.

The seven-bedroom home will also have 3,567 square feet of outdoor living space. Interior highlights will include a great room with a 12-foot ceiling, an entertainer’s kitchen with a quartzite waterfall island, a spacious dining room, a home office, and dual primary suites, each with its own deck and fireplace.

A white oak floating staircase will descend to the lower level, which will have a lounge, a bar, a 172-bottle wine storage system, a golf and sports room, a fitness center with a teak sauna, three guest suites, and a bunk room with access to a patio with an eight-person hot tub and fire pit.

The home will also have an oversized three-car garage with EV-charging capabilities.

Kristen Barber of Stein Eriksen Realty Group is the listing agent.

Old Marsh Golf Club: This legacy compound, set on 1.77 lakefront acres, has a primary residence, a guest cottage, a separate apartment, and a seven-car garage.

The eight-bedroom estate has marble and wood flooring, coffered ceilings, four fireplaces, a commercial elevator, built-ins, and French doors that open to spacious patios.

Other amenities include four fully equipped kitchens, a primary suite with dual dressing rooms and separate baths, a 3,000-bottle wine gallery, an entertainment room, and a bar. Covered patios overlook mature banyan trees, manicured gardens, and a swimming pool.

The property also has access to a Pete Dye championship course, regarded for its no-tee-time policy and renowned caddie program.

Jim McCann of Premier Estate Properties is the listing agent.

The Bluffs at Mauna Kea Resort: Set in an enclave of just 11 oceanfront residences, this five-bedroom estate sits on a promontory with more than 200 feet of ocean frontage.

Overlooking Mauna Kea and Hapuna beaches, the nearly 1-acre lot on Hawai’i’s Kohala Coast is surrounded by Pacific Ocean views. Built in 2004, the 9,909-square-foot home was “refined over six years” by Mexican architects Ricardo and Victor Legorreta, with living spaces designed around light, color, and water.

Glass walls and lanais connect the indoor living areas with the coastal setting. A sunken courtyard with a black stone water feature is surrounded by patios for entertaining and enjoying the views.

The property also has a two-car garage and access to nearby golf, beaches, and dining.

Harold Clarke of Luxury Big Island is the listing agent.

The Light House: This newly built waterfront estate is owned by powerboat racer and real estate investor Ross Romash and his wife, Caroline Romash, who bought the property for $2.45 million in 2021.

Located in the double guard-gated Belle Meade Island, the shimmering six-bedroom home sits on a corner lot with 120 feet of frontage overlooking Biscayne Bay. The double-height great room has disappearing glass walls that transform the space into a waterfront pavilion. The 11,358-square-foot interior includes a “showpiece” kitchen with a waterfall island and a hidden pantry, a primary suite with a water-facing terrace, and a glass-enclosed fitness center with a private poolside entrance and a bay-view sauna.

The nearly half-acre lot also has a summer kitchen, an air-conditioned eight-car auto showroom with glass garage doors, an infinity pool, a spa, a cold plunge, and a private boat dock.

Chad Carroll of Compass is the listing agent.

Intracoastal Waterway views: This seven-bedroom waterfront estate offers 1.88 acres of seclusion, complete with a private beach area.

Built in 1962, the 7,958-square-foot residence also offers the potential to expand by an additional 2,486 square feet, according to the listing. Oversized windows, retractable glass walls, built-in cabinetry, French doors, vaulted ceilings, and private guest suites are featured throughout the home.

Multiple patios provide picturesque views of the pool.

Adrian Reed of William Raveis Real Estate is the listing agent.

Under construction: This six-bedroom Old Cutler estate spans 10,260 square feet of luxurious waterfront living and is slated for completion in fall 2027.

According to The Real Deal, the property is owned by an LLC managed by Mocca Group’s Alex Pirez and Alicia Nussdorf, founder and CEO of hair removal company Completely Bare.

Floor-to-ceiling glass walls frame sweeping views of Biscayne Bay, while formal and casual living, dining, and entertaining spaces are found throughout the home. Upstairs, an ornate primary suite features a private bay-facing terrace, accompanied by four en suite bedrooms.

The property also boasts a wellness center with a gym, sauna, steam room, and cold plunge. Covered terraces overlook the pool and private boat dock.

Elyse Taylor of Mocca Realty is the listing agent.

Estate Section: This rebuilt lakefront residence was sold just two years ago for $51,631,500 to an undisclosed buyer. The sellers were Jeanne S. Siegel and her late husband, investor and businessman Herbert J. Siegel.

The nine-bedroom estate, designed by Marion Sims Wyeth, was reconstructed by the Siegels, who were also involved in a water-damage lawsuit alleging that the company that sold them the home failed to disclose its knowledge of mold caused by water intrusion.

The extensively restored interior features a bright, airy open floor plan framed by sweeping waterfront views. Outside, formal gardens and expansive lawns lead to 165.9 feet of Intracoastal Waterway frontage.

A pool, a guest house, and a deep-water dock are also located on the 1.04-acre property.

Suzanne Trapani- Frisbie of The Corcoran Group is the listing agent.

La Gorce Island: This eight-bedroom waterfront estate is reportedly owned by Betsy Sherman, wife of George Sherman, the late former chairman of the Campbell’s Company.

Built in 2015, the 18,213-square-foot mansion occupies a prominent point lot at the tip of the island, with 320 feet of open Biscayne Bay frontage. Designed to capture waterfront views from nearly every room, the property was customized for both grand entertaining and intimate waterfront living.

Highlights include a primary retreat with a bayfront balcony, a living room with retractable glass walls, and a chef’s kitchen with access to a covered terrace. A floating circular staircase leads to an upstairs primary suite overlooking the bay and pool.

The 1.23-acre property also features a four-car garage and a deep-water dock capable of accommodating large yachts.

Jill Hertzberg of Coldwell Banker Realty is the listing agent.
