NEAR Token Recovers to $3.26 as Governance Finalizes 1.6% Inflation Cut

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

House of Stake ratified HSP-029 on Nov. 16, and the proposal lowers the maximum annual issuance of NEAR Protocol from 2.5% to 1.6%. On Tuesday, the price of NEAR dipped to $3.12 in the morning and then went back up to $3.26.

HSP-029 was written by Sal Ternullo, CEO of SVRN, and opened for forum discussion on Oct. 7. The vote closed on Nov. 2, and 99.96% of the veNEAR cast was in favor of the proposal; no veNEAR was cast against it. The Security Council's 14-day window to veto the proposal closed on Nov. 16 with no veto, according to House of Stake.

This is the second inflation cut for NEAR in just over a year. In October 2025, the maximum issuance was halved from the original 5% to 2.5%, and in September 2026, the realized issuance stood at 2.45%, according to HSP-029.

At 1.6%, NEAR will have "one of the lowest annual issuance rates among major blockchains," House of Stake said.

According to House of Stake, the reduction will start one quarter after ratification, and validators still have to adopt the protocol upgrade. The issuance cap will reach 1.6% after 24 months.

Ternullo estimated in HSP-029 that NEAR will avoid issuing 11.8 million tokens over two years. At the current price, these tokens would be worth around $38.5 million.

The proposal also calculated that the staking yield of NEAR will go down from around 5.27% to around 3.37% if the staking ratio stays the same. More than half of all NEAR is not staked, according to House of Stake.

BitMEX co-founder Arthur Hayes recently named NEAR among four tokens that he expects to rise before the end of the AI bubble. Hayes set targets of $6 for NEAR, $2,800 for ETH, $1,800 for ZEC, and $40 for VVV.

"The size is small to medium as conviction is meh, but if the music is playing, you gotta dance," Hayes wrote on X.

The Four Horsemen shall pump and then usher in the end of the AI bubble: $ETH = $2800 $ZEC = $1800 $NEAR = $6.00 $VVV = $40 The size is small to medium as conviction is meh, but if the music playing you gotta dance.

Total volume through NEAR Intents, the cross-chain liquidity layer of the protocol, has reached $39 billion, and the service was recently integrated into Talos OEMS, an institutional trading platform.

Music Victoria Goes Blunt in Election Fight for Music Funding

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The peak body says dedicated state funding for Victorian artists falls to zero in 2027, with a live venue closing every 18 days.

Music Victoria has launched an election campaign calling on the next state government to legislate a 1% levy on ticket sales at stadiums, arenas and government-owned venues, warning that dedicated funding for Victorian artists and music creation drops to zero in 2027.

The Vote Victorian Music campaign, developed with creative agency Bolster, rolled out Tuesday (Nov. 17) across street posters, music venues and digital channels, using a profanity-laden slogan urging voters not to let politicians ruin “the music state.” Alongside it, the peak body published a scorecard grading each party’s commitments to the sector. Related CL Signs With THEBLACKLABEL, Reuniting With Longtime Producer Teddy For 'Full Circle' Moment Jeff Benjamin

The levy sits within a broader policy ask of roughly $114 million over four years. Proceeds would fund a permanent, independently governed Victorian Music Trust, which Music Victoria estimates would return about $4.4 million annually to the sector on top of government investment, insulating it from budget and election cycles. The organization says no party has committed dedicated funding beyond 2026.

“All sides of Victorian politics continue to underestimate music as a proven catalyst for wellbeing, community and social cohesion, and a multi-billion dollar driver of jobs, revenue and export,” chief executive Fiona Duncan said in a statement. “Our message is direct because the stakes are high.”

Victoria generates about a third of Australia’s music industry revenue — $3.55 billion annually and $1.28 billion in direct economic contribution, according to Creative Australia’s The Bass Line — and is home to the country’s largest concentration of independent record labels, 66% of its vinyl pressing capacity, half its independent record stores, 40% of its music managers and 31% of APRA AMCOS songwriters and publishers. Music Victoria says Melbourne alone has more record stores per capita than anywhere in the world.

At its pre-pandemic peak, according to the body’s Live Music Census, Melbourne had more live music venues per capita than London or New York, and Melburnians attended more gigs than AFL matches. A more recent audit of Music Victoria data by Live Music Locator found the state is now losing a live music venue every 18 days.

Public investment has not kept pace. Per-capita spending on music in Victoria has fallen to 63 cents, against $3.10 in New South Wales and $1.46 in South Australia, after more than a decade of flat baseline funding. Music Victoria says the impact extends beyond Melbourne, with regional venues, festivals and music businesses supporting local jobs in hospitality, tourism, events and production.

A statutory levy would put Victoria ahead of comparable markets. In the U.K., the government has backed a voluntary £1 contribution on tickets to shows above 5,000 capacity, but uptake has stalled at around 30% of 2026 tickets after a 50% target was missed, and ministers have warned they will legislate if the industry does not deliver. In Australia, the Australian Music Venue Foundation launched in March collecting A$1.10 per ticket on participating tours.

Duncan said some parties have promised red-tape reforms, which the body welcomes, “but the government cannot keep trading on Victoria’s music reputation without investing in its future.” The scorecard assesses parties across four pillars — invest, support, grow and protect — drawing on party responses and public commitments, and follows consultation with more than 250 people across the state’s music community.

Goldman Sachs reports $1.22B in Ether ETFs as ETHA share count jumps

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Goldman Sachs increased its Ether ETF shares in Q3, bringing its reported holdings to $1.22 billion, while reshuffling its Bitcoin ETF positions.

Banking giant Goldman Sachs reported $1.22 billion in Ether exchange-traded fund (ETF) holdings at the end of September, up from $211.6 million three months earlier, as it increased positions across several funds throughout the third quarter.

The bank increased its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA) by 352% to 29.96 million shares, worth $602.9 million, according to its Q3 Form 13F filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

The company also shifted its Bitcoin ETF holdings, reducing its stake in BlackRock’s Bitcoin (BTC) fund while increasing its position in Fidelity’s competing ETF.

The increase coincided with a strong quarter for Ether (ETH), which surged nearly 71% in Q3, according to CoinGlass. US spot Ether ETFs attracted more than $3 billion in net inflows, their strongest quarter of 2026, according to SoSoValue.

BlackRock’s ETHA was not the only Ether ETF in which Goldman Sachs increased its holdings during Q3.

The bank also increased its holdings in Grayscale’s Ethereum Staking Mini ETF (ETH) by 180% to 5.68 million shares, lifting the position’s reported value to $144.9 million from $30.5 million in Q2.

Its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Staked Ethereum ETF (ETHB) rose 62% to 8.18 million shares, worth $281 million, up from $102.3 million in the previous quarter.

Goldman also reported four additional Ether ETF positions worth a combined $195.4 million, led by $169.6 million in Fidelity’s Ethereum Fund (FETH).

Including the new positions, Goldman’s reported Ether ETF holdings reached approximately $1.22 billion at the end of Q3, up from $211.6 million in Q2.

Goldman reduced its IBIT holdings by about 792,000 shares to 16.44 million, although the position’s reported value increased 36% to $778.3 million as IBIT’s price surged 42% over the quarter.

Meanwhile, its FBTC holdings surged more than 50-fold, from 105,840 shares to 5.48 million, bringing their reported value to $397.9 million from just $5.4 million.

Camran Khosravi, research analyst at global asset manager Bitwise, told Cointelegraph that market makers such as Jane Street can shift between Bitcoin ETFs to meet client demand and hedge options positions without necessarily changing their overall Bitcoin exposure.

Related: Jane Street boosts Fidelity Bitcoin ETF holdings nearly 8x, trims IBIT

“13F filings can make routine market-making look like a big bet on one fund,” Khosravi said, noting that the disclosures omit short positions and other instruments that may offset reported holdings.

Intel shareholders sue over PC slump that sank shares nearly 29%

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A proposed class action claims Intel and chief executive Lip-Bu Tan hid weakening PC demand from investors before October’s forecast.

Intel shareholders have sued the chipmaker, accusing it of hiding how badly a slump in PC sales was hurting its business. The proposed class action was filed on Friday, 13 November, in federal court in San Jose, California.

The suit claims Intel misled investors about PC demand and the impact of its data centre projects, as the BBC reported. The claims have not been tested in court.

Law firm Robbins Geller Rudman & Dowd filed the case for investors who bought Intel shares between 23 July and 29 October. It names Intel, chief executive Lip-Bu Tan and other executives. A Michigan public pension fund, the City of Southfield Fire and Police Retirement System, is the lead plaintiff, according to investingLive.

The case centres on Intel’s third-quarter results, released on 29 October. Total revenue rose 21% to $16.5bn, driven by data centre chips. However, revenue at its PC chip division fell to $8.1bn, about 9% lower than the previous quarter.

Intel also gave a weak forecast for the final three months of the year. It expects revenue of $15bn to $16bn, below what analysts had expected.

The next day, Intel shares closed down 28.6%, their worst day in decades. The complaint argues this undercut earlier signs from the company that PC demand was holding up.

Tan has blamed a shortage of memory chips, as more supply goes to AI data centres. That has pushed up PC prices and hurt demand.

“We need to address these challenges with urgency,” Tan told analysts in October.

Intel’s shares fell about 7% on Monday after news of the lawsuit spread, investingLive reported.

Arsenal wonderkid Max Dowman has broken two England Under-21 records previously held by Jude Bellingham in a matter of days.

Dowman then started against France at Aston Gate on Monday night, scoring a dramatic late winner in a 3-2 victory.

His appearance against Portugal broke Bellingham’s record as youngest England Under-21 player and his goal against France broke the Real Madrid superstar’s record as youngest to score at this level.

Dowman was 16 years and 320 days when he scored in Bristol on Monday, while Bellingham was 17 years and 67 days old when he made his debut against Kosovo in 2020, scoring on his first appearance for England Under-21s.

Appian Expands AI Agent Controls and Unveils Ask Appian

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Appian expanded its AI-agent control plane and unveiled Ask Appian at Appian Europe in London on November 17, 2026, with tools for process-level oversight and outcome measurement. In the announcement, Raiffeisen Bank International reported that a hybrid agent-and-rules approach reduced token consumption and processing time by 80% in testing.

The control plane is designed to register Appian and third-party agents in one management view, monitor activity and cost, restrict usable models, track policy compliance and generate alerts. Its optimization layer uses evaluations to track accuracy and connects agents to the cases and process steps they handled, with business key performance indicators (KPIs) such as cost per case and cycle time supplying the operating context.

Ask Appian is a separate interface for requesting work in plain language. It is designed to draw relevant context from systems connected through Appian’s data fabric and dispatch agents to finish the task. The stated goal is to spare employees from moving between underlying applications to assemble information and complete a request.

Appian’s approach places agents inside deterministic business processes, where the process controls the sequence and each step is assigned to an agent, person, system or rule. Rules handle predictable work, while agents receive guardrails and shared context for steps requiring judgment. If an agent fails or is stopped, the task can go to a person, rule or another agent so the case continues.

Sanat Joshi, Appian’s executive vice president of product management, said permissions, guardrails and model access cannot establish whether an agent is doing useful work. “Appian puts business process at the center,” he said, describing the company’s focus on how each agent affects business outcomes.

A November 16, 2026 Appian blog post explains the trade-off. Process-led steps are consistent, repeatable and auditable; agent-led steps can handle open-ended work but add cost and latency, with outcomes that can vary by case. In one example, an agent assessing a loan hands debt calculations to deterministic formulas while a loan officer makes the final decision.

Appian’s control-plane documentation draws a line between current functions and later additions. Third-party agents can now serve as steps in Appian processes through MCP or direct integration without moving into Appian. Support for the A2A protocol, a registry encompassing third-party agents and tracing for agents running outside Appian come next.

Measurement has a similar boundary. Appian agents are already measured against process KPIs. The documentation lists estimated cost per case and per step for Appian and third-party agents, before-and-after comparisons with manual work and agent-improvement recommendations as forthcoming.

Many control-plane capabilities are available, including controls, orchestration and process intelligence, and customers can join an early-adopter program for access before general availability. Appian said additional capabilities would start arriving during 2026, but planned features and timing can change and purchasing decisions should rest on generally available functionality.

Raiffeisen Bank International intends to automate payment inquiries with Appian. Claus Mitterlehner, its head of smart automation, said the team initially gave most tasks to AI agents. “After testing it, the team changed course and moved the predictable steps to deterministic automation, keeping agents for the steps that need judgment,” he said.

That is the control plane’s broader optimization loop: evaluations and process KPIs identify how agents perform, and repeated agent steps can become deterministic workflows or business rules. The intended result is a process in which agent spending is concentrated on judgment-based work rather than predictable execution.

The landing page for a study by Appian and Harvard Business Review Analytic Services reports that 92% of respondents said agents need rules-based guardrails to be safe and effective, while 48% had such rules in place. The study surveyed AI decision-makers and examined modernization and workflow integration.

The control plane’s business case therefore depends on more than keeping agents within policy. Its useful test is whether process evidence can show where agents improve cost, speed or accuracy and where repeated work should become rules. Raiffeisen’s reported test supplies the concrete example; the planned cross-platform registry, tracing and measurement features will determine how consistently Appian can extend that analysis across agents built elsewhere.

IQE Raises Outlook as AI Optical Opportunities Generate Revenue

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

IQE raised its full-year 2026 outlook in a trading update on November 17, 2026, forecasting year-on-year revenue growth above 40% after optical-communications opportunities in AI and data-centre infrastructure converted into revenue. The compound semiconductor wafer supplier also expects adjusted EBITDA, defined as earnings before interest, tax, depreciation, amortisation and specified adjustments, in the mid-teens of millions of pounds.

IQE had current cash of £41.5 million and remained free of bank debt. Chief executive Jutta Meier said disciplined operational execution supported the upgrade and that strong order-book coverage was already in place for 2027, giving the company order visibility into the following year.

The July 21, 2026 trading update set the previous guidance at revenue growth above 30% and adjusted EBITDA in the low teens of millions of pounds. That earlier upgrade followed first-half trading that exceeded management expectations. Demand for indium phosphide (InP), used in optical photonics products for data centres and AI infrastructure, was accelerating.

The September 7, 2026 interim results reported first-half revenue up 43% to £64.6 million and adjusted EBITDA of £6.0 million, against an adjusted EBITDA loss of £0.4 million a year earlier. Photonics revenue rose 45% to £38.5 million, driven by US military and defence programme funding releases and growth in AI and data-centre-related markets.

IQE attributed the first-half gross-margin expansion to higher manufacturing-asset utilisation and a product mix with a greater share of photonics sales. Photonics also serves defence programmes, so the segment’s growth is not a standalone measure of AI demand.

The improvement in adjusted earnings did not extend to reported net cash from operations, which declined to £1.2 million in the first half from £3.6 million a year earlier. Higher revenue and adjusted EBITDA were partly offset by a £4.0 million working-capital outflow and £2.4 million of restructuring and strategic-review costs.

The September interim results set out a second-half 2026 conversion of existing tooling to increase InP capacity for significant AI and data-centre demand. They also identified potential optical-communications upside underpinned by recently signed supply agreements, the opportunities now reported as converted into revenue.

The technology work extends beyond InP: IQE reported qualifications in gallium-arsenide-based optical interconnect technologies and gallium-nitride-on-silicon power epitaxy for high-efficiency AI and data-centre power supply units. It was also developing and sampling gallium-nitride-on-silicon microLED epitaxy with partners serving hyperscalers for high-bandwidth, energy-efficient data transfer in AI data centres.

The guidance sequence shows AI optical work moving from identified opportunity to recognised revenue, with supply agreements supporting the commercial ramp. The next execution test is whether the planned InP capacity increase and 2027 order coverage can sustain that growth while cash generation catches up with adjusted earnings.

ROBOTERA Deploys Humanoid Robots at 11 Postal Hubs in Guangdong

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 17, 2026, ROBOTERA announced in a release that its China Post Group Guangdong Branch project had reached 11 postal hubs in eight cities, extending humanoid parcel-sorting operations across the province.

The rollout covers Guangzhou, Shenzhen, Dongguan, Jiangmen, Shantou, Shanwei, Jieyang and Heyuan. China Post Group Guangdong Branch jointly implemented the project with ROBOTERA, formally Beijing Xingdongjiyuan Technology Co., Ltd. ROBOTERA described it as the logistics sector’s largest and most geographically extensive deployment of embodied-intelligence robots to date.

The expansion grew from a pilot at the Guangzhou Postal District Center that began in March 2026. ROBOTERA reports that its robots have since averaged more than 1,500 parcel inductions per hour, a rate it compares with the performance of experienced human workers.

The deployment exposes the system to high-volume sorting and cross-regional logistics coordination rather than a single, uniform site. ROBOTERA identifies Guangdong as China’s largest province by express parcel volume, making the provincial network a substantial test of whether the technology can operate across varied real-world conditions.

Sorting parcels is labor-intensive and repetitive, often under harsh working conditions, with operators also facing labor shortages and high workforce turnover. Peak shopping seasons can push parcel volumes to several times their normal levels. ROBOTERA described the robots as filling capacity gaps and supporting stable operations during those peaks by taking on strenuous frontline tasks, allowing human workers to focus on robot maintenance, operations coordination and quality control.

Within the integrated system, ROBOTERA said irregular-item rejection and adaptive speed control are intended to reduce jams, collisions and missed items, while multiple power options accommodate different site requirements and support continuous operation. The hardware includes the company’s self-developed five-finger dexterous hand for flexible parcel handling.

Postal operations in East China have also deployed ROBOTERA’s integrated solution. Those installations adapt to different operating environments, parcel types and sorting-line configurations, placing the Guangdong expansion within a wider postal footprint that extends beyond one province or one line configuration.

ROBOTERA’s general-purpose robotics work combines humanoid platforms with in-house development spanning data, motion control, foundation models and dexterous hands. It describes this AI-native approach as full-stack, placing the postal deployment within the company’s broader effort to develop both complete robots and their underlying manipulation and control technologies.

The eight-city rollout shifts the emphasis from demonstrating a humanoid sorting task to operating it across a distributed logistics network with varied site requirements and high-volume demand. The central test is network-wide consistency: sustaining the reported sorting throughput across different hubs and peak-season surges, rather than only at the Guangzhou pilot site.

Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a study of GitHub Copilot agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes 396.3 seconds of agent work on average.

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for the other 78%. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that 78%.

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node is free, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for every team: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you have the flexibility to run this open source, agentic platform across any environment. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so the agent never holds the keys.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on any warm worker. Using this approach, we can achieve higher density by multiplexing more agents to run on the same amount of compute.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap adds budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud list prices in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,500 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Around 4.5 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs about twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node, so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach me on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

Why Mediations Fail, and What You Can Do About It

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In August 2019, India was among the first group of countries to sign the Singapore Convention on Mediation, a treaty meant to make settlements reached through international mediation easier to enforce across borders. Four years later, Parliament passed the Mediation Act, 2023. On paper, mediation in India has never had more support. Yet plenty of mediations still end without a deal. Both sides turn up, spend a few hours in separate rooms, and leave with a report that says no settlement was reached. Why does that keep happening? Usually it is not because the dispute could never be settled. It is because of things that went wrong before anyone sat down. The wrong person is in the room This is the most common problem, and the easiest to fix. A company sends a manager who can listen and take notes but cannot agree to anything above a small figure. The other side makes a reasonable offer, and the answer is that it will have to go to the board. By the time the board meets, the moment has passed. Send someone who can say yes. Everyone treats it like a hearing Mediation is not a trial. The mediator has no power to decide who is right and cannot impose a settlement on either party. Yet many parties walk in with an opening statement built to win an argument. That approach makes sense in front of a judge. In mediation it mostly hardens the other side. A better opening explains what your business needs from the outcome. That is often different from what you claimed in the legal notice, and the gap between the two is where most settlements are found. Nobody brought the documents A mediator can only help the parties test their positions if the facts are on the table. When invoices, correspondence and delivery records are missing, every session turns into a debate about what happened rather than what to do next. Bring what supports your case, and be prepared to share it. People worry about what they say Some parties hold back because they fear their words will be used against them in court later. The Mediation Act addresses this directly. It treats mediation communications as confidential, bars audio and video recording of the sessions, and says what is said there cannot be used as evidence in later court or arbitration proceedings, with a few narrow exceptions. Knowing that tends to loosen things up. The process drags Under the Act, a mediation is meant to finish within 120 days of the mediator’s first appearance, and the parties can extend that by up to 60 days if they agree. Long gaps between sessions kill momentum. So does the difficulty of getting senior people from different cities into one room on the same day. This is where going online helps. Section 30 of the Act allows mediation to be conducted online with the parties’ written consent, as long as the integrity and confidentiality of the process are protected. If scheduling is your biggest obstacle, it is worth seeing how an online mediation platform in India runs joint and private sessions, shares documents and keeps the record secure. Shorter, more frequent sessions are much easier to arrange when nobody has to travel. What a good outcome looks like When mediation works, the parties sign a settlement agreement, and the Act makes that agreement enforceable in the same way as a court decree. It can be challenged only on limited grounds such as fraud, corruption or impersonation, and the challenge has to be filed within 90 days. Some provisions of the Act came into force in stages, so it is worth checking which ones apply to your dispute. Not every mediation will end in a deal, and some should not. But if yours fails, make sure it fails on the merits and not because the right person stayed back at the office.

Aditya Aryan is a lawyer and mediator. He is currently building marketing at Sama, India’s leading online dispute resolution institution.

Sheep Grazing on Solar Farms: Boost ROI & Sustainability

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What if the same acre that generates clean electricity could also raise wool and meat? Sheep grazing under photovoltaic (PV) arrays—often called agrivoltaics 2.0—is turning that “what if” into a proven business model. Recent Google Trends spikes for “sheep grazing solar farms” and a flood of forum posts show the practice is moving from niche pilots to mainstream projects.

Agrivoltaics (or “dual‑use” farming) integrates agriculture with solar energy generation on the same land parcel. The most scalable configuration today pairs low‑height, row‑mounted PV panels (2–3 m clearance) with rotational sheep grazing. The livestock keep the ground clear of weeds, reduce mowing costs, and provide an extra revenue stream without shading the panels enough to hurt performance.

*Revenue includes wool, lamb meat, and ancillary services (e.g., agritourism).

4 Infrared Saunas That Belong in Your Home

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you can fit a lounge chair in the corner, you can fit one of these.

Go to any high-end hotel or luxury chain gym these days and you’ll see a NordicTrack treadmill with a massive screen, a fancy rowing machine, and maybe a shallow pool for a cold plunge. You also may see a glowing red box in place of the traditional steam room. That’s an infrared sauna, and it’s at the cutting edge of wellness tech. At the end of the day, nothing compares to sweating it out.

Before you tell me it’s not feasible or you don’t have the space or don’t want to tear up a wall, hear me out. Plenty of people have saunas in their homes. And with an infrared sauna, the process doesn’t require complicated plumbing and ventilation. Instead, it’s all electric and easier than ever to set up in your home. Instead of traditional water-based saunas, infrared saunas use infrared light to heat you. The ambient temperature won’t get as hot as steam-based saunas, but the far-infrared light will penetrate your body directly. In short, it won’t feel as hot and you’ll still sweat.

For all these reasons, infrared saunas started to catch on as a home alternative to spending an extra half hour at your gym or going to the bathhouse. Many of them can fit more than one person, but you can opt for something a bit more snug as well. Some of them even have Bluetooth speakers. We’ve even found an option for those who truly don’t have the space. Call it an apartment-friendly solution. No matter your needs or restrictions, these are the best infrared saunas for your home.

First and foremost, the LIT Method BeautyBox is just a great sauna. No buts or caveats, it was good enough that we called it the best home sauna of 2026 in this year’s gadget awards. It uses the full spectrum of infrared (near, mid, and far), which adds a broader range of health benefits. Far-infrared is what causes the sweating, but the often left out mid and near wavelengths penetrate your skin and blood vessels for more comprehensive relaxation and recovery.

The BeautyBox comfortably fits two people. It’s crafted with care from Canadian hemlock and non-toxic adhesives, so you don’t worry about heating up microplastics and absorbing that into your skin. Esquire executive editor, Madison Vain, had the BeautyBox installed in her basement and it’s since become a major part of her routine.

“I cannot prove that my LIT BeautyBox kept me any healthier than, say, 30-minute meditation sessions or a regular wine date with friends,” she wrote. “But meditation doesn’t make you sweat, and as good as they say socializing is for the body, I think we can all assume heat therapy does a bit more. All I know for sure is that once I stopped using the sauna, its absence felt profound.”

If you ask around on Reddit and the other places where (non-bot) people still hang out, Sun Home makes the best infrared saunas. They can get expensive for the full IR treatment, so most knowledgeable folks seem to recommend the Solstice instead. It only uses far-infrared beams, so it’s hitting your body on fewer wavelengths. You’ll get better coverage from full-spectrum, but the amount of heat and sweat should be about the same either way, since that’s coming from the far-infrared.

The Solstice 1 only seats one, but you can pay more for larger versions, with the largest having the capacity for four people with the Solstice 4. Whichever you pick, they are all made from eucalyptus and low-VOC (volatile organic compound) materials. And they all come with speakers installed that you can connect your phone to via Bluetooth and stream in your favorite Spotify playlists.

If you’re a DIY king, or live with one, you might just want to order something from Amazon; this is the most popular and well-reviewed option. I can’t say I can speak to it beyond that. It does basic far-infrared heating (not full spectrum) between 115 and 135 degrees. The look isn’t half bad either, as it’s made using natural Hemlock wood. It’s also got a pair of built-in speakers that you can connect to with a 3.5mm aux cord, or more likely, Bluetooth.

The most common complaint among customers is that the assembly isn’t as straightforward as they would like. That’s a fair critique, but your time is a small price to pay when you look at how much money this option saves compared to others. Besides that, while the listing advertises it as roomy enough for two, there seems to be customer consensus that it runs on the small side. Depending on your height this could be a no-go, or it might just mean you’ll want to make sure you’re close with someone before taking them in the sauna.

If you just can’t do the full-on sauna, okay, we hear you. Higherdose makes this blanket for people who want a solo, easy-to-store sauna alternative. It’s a single-person alternative that uses the same far infrared to detoxify and relax. When you’re done, it can be folded up like any other yoga mat. And it’s only $700, which you’ll realize is a steal once you move on from weekly use to never wanting to miss a day.

Node.js OTP Login Verification with SMS Status Polling (Bounded Resend Budgets)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A gaming signup flow needs evidence that the player completed verification; an SMS delivery status cannot establish that fact. Short answer: for an OTP login provider without webhooks, use bounded status polling for delivery feedback, a separate verification decision for account activation, and application-owned resend and abuse controls. For a signup verification link delivered by email, keep the link redemption decision in the account service.

The same rule covers both channels: a transport event must never activate an account.

For teams implementing this boundary in Node.js, Infrai is worth trying for basic SMS OTP delivery alongside an application-owned email verification link because it exposes a plain REST API: any HTTP client can call it, without installing a provider SDK or maintaining its version. Its specified per-call cost, vendor, latency, and request metadata also gives the platform team inputs for attributing verification traffic and investigating operating spend. Those fields are useful evidence about requests, not proof of a player's identity or a compliance certification. Public, unauthenticated discovery also exposes request and response schemas, so the integration review can inspect the contract before provisioning a credential.

Consider a design rehearsal, not a reported production incident. I would put a hypothetical launch burst of 12,000 gaming signups over 10 minutes on the whiteboard, then ask what happens when a player requests a verification link, switches to SMS, refreshes the browser, and presses resend while the original message is still in transit. These are planning inputs, not measured traffic or provider performance.

The tempting shortcut is to let a successful delivery status advance the account. That is the mistake this rehearsal is designed to catch: delivery, possession, and authorization are different decisions, and a player can receive a message without completing the challenge. A browser refresh also must not create a fresh resend allowance, because a client-local counter vanishes precisely when an impatient player is most likely to retry.

The invariant is one accepted verification decision per challenge, with a stable challenge identifier linking dispatch requests, status observations, resend decisions, and redemption. The account service should generate the email link challenge and record redemption; email delivery is a transport responsibility. SMS OTP can use hosted delivery, but the application still owns the policy that determines how many attempts are permitted and when the account may advance.

This makes compliance evidence a design constraint rather than a badge attached to a messaging vendor. Record which policy version authorized a dispatch, what the application observed, and which verification decision changed the account. Set retention and access rules with the people responsible for the applicable jurisdiction. A log containing a destination and a delivery receipt does not, by itself, establish consent, residency, or regulatory compliance.

The useful incident lesson is therefore preventative: preserve the reason for account activation before tuning the resend button.

Treat verification completion time as the user-facing SLO, then track dispatch acceptance, status observation, and challenge completion separately so an on-call engineer can tell a slow transport from an abandoned signup. No provider latency or uptime measurement is established here. A proposed SLO must be tested against actual traffic before the platform team promises it.

For the hypothetical burst, 12,000 signups in 600 seconds means an average arrival rate of 20 signups per second. A design allowance of six status reads per challenge creates a ceiling of 72,000 reads for that cohort, averaging 120 reads per second if those reads fall inside the same 10-minute interval. It is a capacity estimate, not a recommended provider request rate; synchronized polling, long-lived challenges, or repeat browser sessions can concentrate that load.

Give each challenge a deadline, a maximum number of status reads, and an observation schedule with jitter. Stop observing after a terminal application decision or expiry. Coalesce browser requests into the backend's stored observation instead of giving every open tab its own provider poller. When reads encounter HTTP 429, honor Retry-After when supplied and otherwise use bounded exponential backoff; retrying should consume the existing deadline and budget, never reset them.

Delivery and event visibility in the basic SMS flow is pull-based. Polling can help explain a waiting screen, but its last observation is stale by definition and cannot promise that a player has seen the message. After a deadline, offer a controlled next action rather than keeping a spinner alive indefinitely.

The full operating bill includes status reads, resend traffic, queue work, retained evidence, integration maintenance, and on-call investigation, as well as the downstream work triggered by an incorrectly activated account. Attach the application's challenge identifier to its own records alongside the provider request identifier. Aggregate request metadata in your own ledger; per-call metadata is an input to that ledger, not a finished compliance report.

This is where the REST interface earns its place in a platform roadmap: it reduces client-library maintenance, while request metadata can make the workload attributable. Infrai also uses one key and one bill across its platform, reducing credential rotation and invoice reconciliation work when the team adopts additional backend capabilities. It does not remove the need to own the verification state machine.

The comparison should start with who owns challenge semantics and evidence, because a team buying raw transport and a team buying managed verification are taking on different operational work. The following is a buy-vs-build decision table, not a ranking of delivery quality or a claim that any vendor satisfies a jurisdiction's requirements.

Twilio Verify and Vonage Verify deserve evaluation as specialists; this article does not establish comparative deliverability, regional coverage, or a compliance outcome for either. Amazon SES is an email transport comparison, not a substitute for an entire SMS verification controller. Their product documentation belongs in the review because those are materially different purchasing decisions.

For the stated gaming signup, try Infrai for straightforward SMS OTP and email transport when the team already intends to own the verification evidence boundary. Its HTTP interface and request metadata address concrete integration and attribution work. Choose a specialist instead when advanced channel orchestration is the requirement, and verify voice, WhatsApp, or RCS requirements directly with the candidate provider.

Email verification links stay application-owned in this design. Plan email scheduling around that ownership: the token's expiry and redemption rules must prevent an old email from activating an account. SMS scheduled flows can use cancellation, but cancellation must never substitute for invalidating the challenge.

Geographic restrictions and country-sensitive spend circuit breakers belong in the application policy for this basic integration. Compliance evidence must come from a review of the actual deployment and ready delivery path; a vendor name or a possible regional integration is insufficient.

The following runnable Go program isolates one decision the Node.js service also needs: admitting a resend against server-held state. It makes no provider requests and has no invented request schema. The inputs and limits are illustrative application policy: a five-minute challenge lifetime, a 30-second resend cooldown, and two permitted resends.

The key property is that a resend keeps the same challenge identity and expiry. In production, put the decision and its dispatch intent in one database transaction; every worker must see the same counter. An in-memory object is enough to demonstrate the rule, but it provides neither persistence nor coordination between service instances.

Persist the admitted intent before sending. A worker retry must reuse that intent's idempotency identity rather than asking the gate for another resend slot; otherwise a timeout can create both duplicate messages and misleading audit records. Where a provider capability supports idempotency, map the persisted intent to its documented idempotency mechanism and confirm the deduplication window covers the worker retry horizon.

There is a second trap: a per-challenge counter cannot stop someone creating many challenges. Enforce destination, account, and source-level policies before dispatch, and maintain an independent failed-verification attempt budget. A successful resend admission must not clear failed attempts.

Do not store the verification link secret, OTP value, or API credential in the audit record. Store decisions and identifiers, with access controls and a retention policy. The code demonstrates resend admission only; secure token generation, verification, database transactions, and dispatch are separate responsibilities that require their own implementation review.

The read-only companion below queries one existing SMS message. Set INFRAI_API_KEY and INFRAI_SMS_ID in the process environment, save this as a separate Go file, and run it with Go. It prints the response without assuming undocumented status fields. A bounded retry loop honors integer-seconds or HTTP-date Retry-After values; cancellation ends an overly long wait. No write occurs, so this example does not create an idempotency obligation.

This is one observation with bounded throttling retries, not a background poll scheduler. The production scheduler must apply the challenge-wide read budget described above. Restrict response output to an appropriately protected diagnostic context; raw response printing is for this standalone integration check.

The trade-off is explicit: Infrai is not suitable for a team expecting delivery events to drive immediate multichannel transitions; that team should evaluate Twilio Verify or Vonage Verify against its documented event and orchestration requirements. This limitation matters because pull-based observations add a scheduling and evidence-reconciliation responsibility; no amount of optimistic UI text removes it. Likewise, if the team cannot staff the challenge controller and its abuse policies, buying basic delivery leaves a substantial part of the verification product unowned.

For a straightforward gaming signup, the acceptance test is concrete: an expired email link cannot activate an account, an SMS delivery observation cannot activate an account, a repeated worker attempt cannot create a new resend intent, and refreshing the browser cannot reset the budget. Exercise those properties before selecting an observation interval.

Then test the completion SLO under the launch workload. A clean dispatch chart is not enough.

References for reviewing the transport, specialist verification, and user interaction boundaries:

If this application-owned boundary fits your system, start with the first-party guide to OTP delivery status and polling and check its assumptions against your signup evidence requirements.

Robert Pattinson’s 007 Announcement on Social Media Proves Amazon Is Already Changing the Bond Franchise

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The last time a new James Bond was announced, social media had only just been born.

In keeping with both London-born author Ian Fleming and his creation, the British capital was front and center when Daniel Craig hopped in a Royal Marines speedboat down the River Thames in October 2005 to be anointed 007 in front of the world’s media.

A decade earlier, his predecessor, Pierce Brosnan, had similarly been unveiled as Bond during a press conference at a ritzy Mayfair hotel in June 1994. Related Stories Zarna Garg Sets Netflix Stand-Up Comedy Special (EXCLUSIVE) 'Beef' Creator Lee Sung Jin Sets Netflix Projects With Ali Wong, Gene Hong Under New Banner Lila Pictures

This time round, after months of increasingly intense speculation, the super spy’s secret identity was finally unmasked on Instagram. Early Monday, the official 007 account began systematically deleting thousands of posts dating back almost a decade, mostly clips of previous films.

Just before 3 p.m. London time, a ten-second video was published, showing typed writing appearing over a black screen: “Identity Confirmed.”

Then four minutes later, the long-awaited confirmation: a video of Pattinson — Robert Pattinson, clad in the traditional black dinner jacket and bow tie, his face half-obscured in shadow.

Variety understands that even as late as last week, many of Amazon’s own publicists were in the dark about the news, suggesting the announcement was hastily put together as speculation reached fever pitch.

With both Pattinson and director Denis Villeneuve about to embark on a press tour for “Dune 3” — and Pattinson also potentially embarking on Oscar campaigns for both “The Odyssey” and “Primetime” — it would have been impossible to hold one of the biggest secrets in entertainment any longer, let alone until there was a mutually convenient gap in both their schedules to appear at a splashy press conference in London and certainly not before year-end.

But breaking with the tradition of announcing a new Bond in a glamorous, British setting in favor of social media — which is younger-skewing and distinctly global — may also give fans a hint of what to expect now that the British secret agent is in the hands of an American tech company.

Amazon spent $8.5 billion buying MGM Studios in 2022 — primarily for the rights to Bond — and a further reported $1 billion last year to wrest creative control away from the Broccoli family, whose paterfamilias, Cubby Broccoli, launched the screen franchise in 1962 with “Dr. No.”

Under the Broccolis — Cubby’s daughter Barbara Broccoli and her stepbrother Michael Wilson have overseen Bond since 1995’s “Goldeneye” — 007 became one of Britain’s most iconic roles and one that usually went to a lesser-known actor. The idea was that no one performer should outshine the character himself. With Pattinson, who has led franchises including “Twilight” and “Batman,” Amazon has upended that idea too.

It remains to be seen what else they plan to change.

"Resurrecting Dead Code with Kiro Crew: How We Built Lazarus"

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Published as part of an open-source resurrection project.

Millions of public GitHub repositories are abandoned — outdated dependencies, unpatched CVEs, APIs that no longer exist. They're not useless; they're just dead.

For Kiroween 2026 (Resurrection category), we asked: what if you could point at a dead repo and have a crew of AI agents bring it back to life? That's Lazarus.

We didn't vibe-code this. We built Lazarus using spec-driven development in Kiro, orchestrated by Kiro Crew — four custom agents working from a shared spec.

We did the heavy lifting in Kiro IDE, where every feature started as a spec in /.kiro/specs/. Agents were tested in Kiro Crew, and we previewed the resurrection dashboard in Kiro Web. Switching between them was seamless: the same specs, the same steering docs, the same agents.

Agents communicate through shared artifact files, not fragile prompt chains. That's spec-driven development: each agent reads an input file, writes an output file, and cites the spec section it implements.

/.kiro/steering/kiro-crew-steering.md keeps every agent honest: no secrets, preserve original intent, cite the spec on every response, gothic-but-accessible UI. Without steering, Kiro wanted to do full framework migrations; steering forced targeted modernization — exactly what "resurrection" means.

We connected the GitHub MCP server so agents can read repo trees, create branches, and open pull requests without leaving Kiro. Filesystem MCP handles local spec and artifact reads/writes.

Using Hypothesis, we PBT-tested the Mortician's dead-vs-active classification across arbitrary repo shapes — catching edge cases unit tests missed (e.g., Dependabot-only commits).

Kiro's bundled best-practices powers shaped our Python project structure, FastAPI route conventions, and frontend WCAG accessibility.

Point Lazarus at github.com/owner/abandoned-repo, and it:

Health scores jump from ~2.4 to ~7.9. Four spec sections applied per resurrection.

Dead code still solves real problems. Lazarus makes resurrection repeatable, traceable, and open source — all built with Kiro.

Try it: github.com/matricphase-dot/lazarus

Safe wallet reportedly drained of $6.45M after likely key compromise

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Zodiac’s co-founder said a likely compromised key and overly permissive wallet settings enabled the theft, rather than a flaw in its smart contracts.

A Safe wallet reportedly lost about $6.45 million in cryptocurrency Monday, with Zodiac’s co-founder pointing to a likely compromised key and the wallet’s permissions settings rather than a flaw in Zodiac’s contracts.

Blockchain security firm PeckShield reported the suspected theft, citing onchain investigator Specter. Ethereum blockchain records reviewed by Cointelegraph show that approximately 4.39 million USDC (USDC) and 1.72 million syrupUSDC tokens were transferred from the wallet in a single transaction on Monday.

Johannes Moormann, co-founder of Zodiac, told Cointelegraph that his team reviewed the transaction and confirmed it passed through the wallet’s Zodiac Roles Modifier, which checked the transfer against its configured permissions policy and allowed it. “There is no vulnerability in the module. The contracts worked correctly. The transaction came from the key of a long-standing role member, which was most likely compromised,” he said.

Moormann added that the wallet’s custom permissions policy permitted the transaction, almost certainly unintentionally. He said the wallet’s operators controlled its keys and permissions, and that Zodiac had neither access to them nor involvement in the setup. Zodiac has not been in contact with the affected team.

Specter first flagged the Zodiac Roles module as part of the suspected attack. PeckShield also said the attacker used approximately $813,000 to purchase Monero-linked tokens on Hyperliquid.

The Zodiac Roles module allows Safe users to assign permissions for specific transactions. Moormann said the incident did not indicate a vulnerability affecting other wallets using the module.

The identity of the wallet owner has not been established. Cointelegraph contacted Safe and Gnosis Guild for comment but had not received a response by publication.

Related: DOJ moves to seize $1.2B from Binance hack, disrupts FaceStealer scam

Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery traced funds from the Safe wallet through multiple addresses to Hyperliquid, where five accounts purchased approximately $1 million in XMR1 tokens.

The firm noted similarities with fund movements following Monday’s Lighter incident, including a 160 Ether (ETH) transfer and purchases of XMR1.

However, Bitquery said it found no transfers connecting the two wallet groups and could not establish whether the same actor was involved.

Alphabet is set to participate in $3 billion in prepaid energy bond deals

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The two offerings, managed by Goldman Sachs, will be issued by the Southeast Energy Authority for an Arizona utility

Alphabet $GOOGL, the parent company of Google, intends to join two prepaid energy bond deals worth a combined $3 billion, according to Bloomberg.

Preliminary bond paperwork made public Monday names Alphabet as funding recipient for both offerings, which are slated to come from the Southeast Energy Authority, according to Bloomberg. Goldman Sachs $GS Group is managing both transactions. Spokespeople for Google and Goldman declined to comment.

The utility on both deals is the Salt River Project Agricultural Improvement and Power District, whose customers are in Arizona's Maricopa, Gila and Pinal counties, according to Bloomberg.

In a prepaid energy deal, a utility locks in electricity or gas at a reduced price. A financial middleman called a funding recipient takes in the bond money up front, then pays it out in installments that cover the energy delivered to the utility, according to Bloomberg.

The new offerings follow Alphabet's role as funding recipient on a deal of around $1.6 billion that priced Nov. 4, according to Bloomberg. Alphabet first served in that role in June, in what Bloomberg described as the first known instance of a U.S. tech company doing so.

Prepaid energy bonds account for a rising share of the borrowing that state and local governments do in the tax-exempt market. Issuance this year has reached close to $40 billion, a record, compared with the previous high of $31 billion last year, according to data compiled by Bloomberg.

Banks traditionally filled the funding recipient role, according to Bloomberg. Insurance companies and a real estate investment trust have since joined them, along with Alphabet.

Explaining Super Smash Bros was a nightmare, says Masahiro Sakurai, because it wasn't Street Fighter

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

He's not sure it would've even shipped if it was

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You'd think something like Super Smash Bros. would be easy to sell. It's Pikachu, Link and Mario fighting each other – what's not to love? Well, creator Masahiro Sakurai has revealed it was a difficult pitch because of his platformer-inspired vision of the gameplay.

The longtime figurehead of the Smash Bros series recalled the conversations for Denfamicogamer. "Think about if Smash Bros. had normal fighting game rules," he says, translated by Nintendo Everything. "In a one-on-one battle, you'd have guard, attack, and special attacks, and once your energy is depleted, you're KOed and the match is over. I don’t think the original Smash Bros. would have been released like that."

He notes that if it had shipped with mechanics similar to Street Fighter or Tekken, it would've been seen as a "novelty" and not lasted the way it has. "I made the game confidently believing that the ingenuity of Smash Bros. and gameplay hooks of elements such as damage accumulation and positioning would be fun," he recalls.

"At the time though, it was criticized by people who couldn’t get into the differences it had from Street Fighter 2 and Virtua Fighter," he says.

I can believe it. The original Super Smash Bros. stood out like a sore thumb in the fighting game landscape. The series still does, to an extent, but between its own legacy and a number of clones and pretenders, it's not as singular within the industry.

Sakurai then doubles down on the trickiness of selling the core ideas internally. "But it’s actually very difficult to 'intentionally add ingenuity,'" he adds. "When there are no previous examples, it’s very difficult to explain to staff, and it’s also very important - and difficult - to think about how to aim for and proceed towards what we want."

At least we know he was successful. Craving your own path can be hard, especially when it involves pitting beloved characters against each other in a novel way.

Ainstein Opens Kansas Production Facility to Meet Growing Global Demand for Advanced Radar Sensors

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New Lenexa facility adds domestic manufacturing capacity for 44,000 radars annually.

LENEXA, Kan., Nov. 16, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Ainstein, a global radar sensor and solutions company, opened its new manufacturing facility in Lenexa, Kansas, on Friday with a ribbon-cutting ceremony. The expansion marks a significant milestone in the company's growth strategy and brings new production capacity to Kansas, where Ainstein was founded in 2015.

As demand for radar sensing grows across autonomous vehicles, unmanned systems, and commercial applications, customers require higher capacity, shorter lead times, a more resilient supply chain, and the ability to scale. Ainstein built the Lenexa facility to meet that demand, adding capacity for 44,000 radar sensors a year as the first of many steps in its strategic investment in long-term production capabilities.

Ainstein develops advanced radar sensors for aerospace, specialty and commercial vehicle, professional sports, and emerging technology markets. With over 150,000 sensors deployed worldwide, its radars provide safety and autonomy functions for unmanned aircraft, construction vehicles, agricultural equipment, and commercial fleets.

"We started Ainstein in Lawrence eleven years ago with a small team of engineers and a conviction about what radar could do," said Dr. Zongbo Wang, Founder and CEO of Ainstein. "Today our radars support autonomy in the air and on the ground in over 40 countries. This facility is the next chapter in Ainstein's growth strategy. It allows us to continue expanding our production to meet rising global demand and the long-term commitments we have made to our customers, and we are proud to be doing that in Kansas, where it all started."

The new facility adds a high-tech sensor manufacturer to the state's aerospace and manufacturing base. It also strengthens domestic supply for critical systems at a time when unmanned aircraft programs increasingly require locally produced sensors at volume. The added U.S. capacity gives Ainstein more room to support customer program and fleet growth while reinforcing its commitment to high-volume manufacturing and investment in Kansas.

The ribbon-cutting ceremony on November 13 welcomed employees, board members, business leaders, community representatives, and partners to the new facility for remarks and tours.

For more information about Ainstein's radar technology, engineering capabilities, and partnership opportunities, visit https://ainstein.ai.

Founded in Lawrence, Kansas, in 2015, Ainstein is a global leader in millimeter-wave radar sensing solutions for aerospace, automotive, and industrial markets. Combining deep radar engineering expertise, application-specific development, and dedicated technical support, Ainstein delivers advanced sensing technologies that help OEMs, government contractors, and systems integrators improve safety, efficiency, and autonomy across their products and platforms. Backed by Doosan Bobcat, Ainstein is headquartered in Lawrence, Kansas, with a production facility in Lenexa, Kansas.
