Sandisk Is Splitting Its Stock 8-for-1. Here’s the Number I’d Check Before Buying.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sandisk’s profit margin matters more to the investment case than its new share count.

A stock split always generates buzz. So, when Sandisk (SNDK -6.90%) recently announced an 8-for-1 stock split, investors cheered.

Yet while stock splits can make headlines, investors may want to focus on another figure -- its gross margin -- rather than its new, lower stock price. Here's why.

First, let's talk about stock splits. While they may seem exotic, they don't actually change anything fundamental about the business. The best analogy is culinary: If you take a whole, uncut pizza and cut it into eight slices, you've done nothing to change how much food you have. You've simply divided it up.

Stock splits work the same way. Fundamentals, like revenue, net income, and free cash flow, do not change when a company splits its shares. When Sandisk shares split on Nov. 20, no one receives anything for free; they will simply see what they already own split according to the 8-for-1 principle.

For example, say you own 10 shares of Sandisk today. As of Nov. 13, each share trades for around $858, for a total market value of $8,580. After the split, someone who owned 10 shares will own 80 shares, and each share will drop to $107.25 (assuming the price stays steady between now and then). The total market value of 80 shares at $107.25 per share? $8,580, the same as before.

Granted, the price of Sandisk shares will fluctuate between now and Nov. 20, but the principle remains the same. The market value of shares going into Nov. 20 will still equal the market value after the split; the only thing that will change is the share price.

Rather than focusing on the stock split, I think investors should take a closer look at Sandisk's gross margins. Gross margin is the profit remaining after deducting the cost of making a product.

Imagine Sandisk sells a memory chip for $100. If it took $15 to physically assemble the product, its gross margin on that product would be 85%. Bear in mind that the company doesn't get to keep the full $85 – it still has administrative and research costs, not to mention taxes.

However, gross margins are useful because they strip away these costs, allowing investors to see how profitable a business is without them. Sandisk's gross margins exploded since 2024, increasing from near 10% to around 85%.

SNDK Gross Profit Margin (Quarterly) data by YCharts

However, the big question is whether the company can maintain these incredible margins, or whether those margins will come crashing down.

For Sandisk, its gross margins are driven more by the price customers are willing to pay than by the input costs for its products. Therefore, it may be helpful to think of Sandisk's business like a hotel. The same rooms are far more profitable at $400 per night than at $150 per night, with many of the hotel's costs remaining steady.

Similarly, the supply-and-demand dynamics of the memory market have an enormous effect on Sandisk's gross margins. If memory demand were to pull back, perhaps as a result of slowing AI infrastructure investment, Sandisk's gross margins could tumble. On the other hand, if the company's long-term memory contracts prove durable, Sandisk's gross margins may remain resilient.

Stock splits alone do not make a stock an attractive investment. The split changes the unit size; profits determine the investment's appeal.

As a result, Sandisk must maintain its impressive gross margins in future quarters in order to justify its appeal. The stock already trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 4.6. That's down significantly from its peak of over 25 in June, but it remains far above its 2025 average multiple of around 1.7.

The market has already priced a major step into Sandisk's profitability. If that fall doesn't materialize, the stock could be primed for a move higher. That's why I'm intrigued by Sandisk's gross margins, not its upcoming stock split.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

The platform is the work of founder and CEO Umut Tugrul, a software engineer with 26 years in the field, who spent four years and thousands of backtests turning institutional-style market analysis into something a phone can explain in one sentence.

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent Just Issued a Blistering Warning to Nvidia

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

During an interview with Fox News over the weekend, Bessent did not hold back about his concerns on the unfolding scandal at Nvidia.

The market seems to have mixed feelings about the departure of former Nvidia (NVDA -3.40%) CEO Jensen Huang.

An independent online petition requesting that Huang be reinstated now has over 180,000 signatures, including those from prominent business leaders like Elon Musk. President Donald Trump has also reportedly said he wouldn't mind if Huang returned as CEO.

During an interview with Fox News over the weekend, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent expressed significant concerns about the situation and called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia. Make no mistake, Bessent just issued a blistering warning to the artificial intelligence (AI) chip giant.

Huang's resignation came after media outlets reported that the Nvidia co-founder had a relationship with a woman named Alice Huang (no relation), who previously was a controlling shareholder in a Singaporean data center company called MegaSpeed.

Other media outlets, such as The New York Times, reported that Huang also had alleged extramarital affairs with Nvidia employees. But the relationship with Alice raises national security concerns.

The Information reported that Alice created a network that illegally diverted $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia chips to China. The U.S. currently has export restrictions on the sale of certain Nvidia chips to China. Nvidia has claimed that it has had virtually zero Chinese chip sales since April 2025.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent said during the Fox interview. "If this were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the OCC (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) would be in there right now, and a lot of the executives would be removed."

Bessent didn't hold back his views on the scandal, either, saying it was clear that some companies buying chips from Nvidia were shell companies working to advance AI interests in China.

Additionally, Bessent clearly does not want to see Huang return as CEO and believes Nvidia needs to get its ducks in a row quickly.

"This makes me think that as the board conducts the search for a new leader, that they need to go outside the company," he said. "I think we need answers in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel."

Bessent has been a close confidant of Trump during his second term. The question is, how much weight does Bessent's warning carry within the administration?

Not only did Trump recently say he wouldn't mind seeing Huang return to Nvidia, but Bessent's questions also suggest divisions between Bessent and Howard Lutnick, another close confidant of Trump and the Secretary of the U.S. Commerce Department.

Bessent told CNBC that some federal agencies wanted to put MegaSpeed on an export blacklist. "But Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

A White House spokesperson told Reuters last week that Trump has "complete confidence" in Lutnick. U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Nov. 12 that there is no confirmation yet that Alice was in fact a Chinese spy.

But questions remain about the extent to which Nvidia's chip sales, particularly in the Asia-Pacific region, have been diverted to China, if at all.

As Treasury Secretary, Bessent doesn't have direct authority to punish Nvidia, although historically he has been considered part of Trump's inner circle and has largely been in lockstep with the president. But with Trump seemingly supporting Huang, it's hard to know how much sway Bessent's words carry.

Still, it's clear that Bessent just issued a blistering warning to Nvidia, and I doubt he is alone among officials in the administration with concerns, so Bessent's warning shouldn't fall on deaf ears.

Anthropic's IPO Takes Place on November 19. Investors Will Learn These 3 Key Details.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic's IPO will be a major event for the entire AI trade.

Anthropic will take center stage on Nov. 19 when it holds its initial public offering (IPO). It will be a critical day for the artificial intelligence (AI) trade, as how the stock is received could indicate whether the party is truly over or if it's full speed ahead.

While investors will be keeping an eye on a number of details connected to Anthropic, these are three of the most important details to monitor.

Companies like Nvidia (NVDA -3.40%) and Micron (MU -6.30%) have achieved excellent financial growth due to soaring hardware sales. The economics of selling semiconductors have been well established.

However, investors do not yet know as much about the economics of selling AI services. While companies like Alphabet (GOOG -2.96%) (GOOGL -3.06%) and Microsoft (MSFT -0.28%) have offered hints about their AI service revenues, these corporations were established long before AI became mainstream.

Anthropic will be the true test since its entire business revolves around AI services like Claude. The demand case for Claude is undeniable, but the big question is whether Anthropic can actually turn a profit with its services.

"We finally get to see what the other side of the equation looks like. What do the economics of selling AI look like? That's a great valuation marker. It's a great referendum," Direxion Head of Capital Markets Jake Behan said at Direxion's "Ahead of the Cycle" live briefing at the New York Stock Exchange.

The revenue growth is certainly there, and that has been the case for a while. Anthropic reported $17 billion in Q3 revenue. That was a 1,127% year-over-year jump, but a 47% sequential increase, which indicates that its growth rate is moderating.

The main concern is that Anthropic booked a $23.3 billion net loss in the third quarter. However, its operating loss of $567 million was more palatable. Investors should get more clarity about how Anthropic’s financial state is being viewed after the IPO, and later when the company reports its Q4 earnings.

The second key insight will be the level of appetite for the newly debuted stock. Anthropic’s IPO will essentially be a measure of whether investors are still willing to take risks as the AI trade continues its prolonged downturn.

That level of demand implies that investors are still eager to get more exposure to the AI trade, despite the current broadly negative sentiment. The amount of capital destined to flow into Anthropic may partially explain why AI stocks have been down in recent weeks. Anthropic is targeting a $1.4 trillion valuation, and all of that money has to come from somewhere.

Anthropic's IPO is generating plenty of buzz, and the oversubscription suggests that an early boom such as the one investors saw with SpaceX (SPCX -1.96%) is possible. The challenge will be whether Anthropic holds on to any early gains or deflates like a balloon.

Investors use metrics like the price-to-earnings ratio, price/earnings-to-growth (PEG) ratio, and price-to-sales ratio to value companies. While the last metric has its flaws, it’s the only one of those that can be applied here since Anthropic is still unprofitable.

While it's easy to weigh the values of chipmakers against each other with those metrics, Anthropic doesn't have publicly traded peers to measure its valuation against — and won’t until OpenAI's IPO, which most expect to happen next year.

Some investors can forgive a young company high net losses if its revenue growth is surging and it’s making progress toward consistent profitability. That generosity will be tested in the days and weeks after Anthropic's IPO.

This year’s previous blockbuster IPO, SpaceX, has been quite the roller coaster ride for shareholders. However, investors were quite happy with its Q3 earnings report, which came out on Oct. 20, and featured 161% year-over-year revenue growth and $4.4 billion in net income.

SpaceX owns the Grok large language model, so it may be a good gauge of how investors approach Anthropic. If Anthropic’s operating losses get more manageable or it transitions to delivering positive net income, that may be enough to offset any concerns that come up.

How Anthropic fares in the weeks following its IPO won't just matter for the company. It will be a vital indicator of the state of the entire AI trade.

Nvidia board is not considering bringing Jensen Huang back as CEO, source tells CNBC

📅 2026-11-16  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia's board of directors is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC.

The board is focused on a investigation into Huang, and is not in discussions to bring him back, the person said. Nvidia has hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead a probe into Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or Nvidia secrets.

Last week, Huang resigned from Nvidia, the company he founded, after 33 years as CEO. The resignation came under a week after Huang had admitted to an affair, and news stories alleged he had an affair with Alice Huang, who worked for a customer and is alleged to have contact with Chinese intelligence, as well as employees at Nvidia.

Over the weekend, the Financial Times said Huang had discussed coming back with allies. Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner told CNBC on Monday that Huang had not discussed a comeback with him.

A representative for Huang declined to comment on speculation on Sunday.

Many in the tech world have called for Huang to return as Nvidia CEO.

An online petition calling for his reinstatement has drawn 180,000 signatures as well as support from major figures including Elon Musk, Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney.

President Trump said on Monday that he is open to Jensen Huang returning to CEO of Nvidia.

"It was not put at risk, and our country is now stronger than ever before," Trump told reporters when asked about U.S. national security concerns.

On Friday, a representative for Nvidia's board said that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the Nvidia CEO role.

'GTA 6' Hacker Pleads Guilty To Extorting Rockstar, Trolling In Prison

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Arion Kurtaj’s hacking antics spoiled GTA 6 and reportedly cost companies millions

Arion Kurtaj, the hacker behind Grand Theft Auto 6's first major leak in 2022, just concluded a plea hearing in front of a London judge. The 21-year-old was accused of multiple counts of unauthorized operation of a computer, fraud, securing access to computer material with intent, and possession of a mobile phone while in prison. Kurtaj, who was a teenager when the crimes were committed, pleaded guilty on all counts.

Kurtaj's court plea may come to a surprise to some, as the hacker was initially deemed unfit to stand trial in 2023. After spending two years in a secure hospital, Kurtaj was reassessed in 2025 and soon moved to prison. The hacker called attention to himself while behind bars by uploading a number of photos on social media in which he mocked the authorities. The Crown Prosecution Service confirmed Kurtaj's possession of an illicit mobile phone in a press release on Monday, in which the prosecutor further detailed his case status.

The hacker published 50 minutes of GTA 6 gameplay split across 90 videos in 2022, all of which depicted a game that was not yet ready for public consumption. In addition to showing standard franchise gameplay mechanics, the leak was responsible for spoiling the series' first playable female character. At the time, Kurtaj threatened to release the game's source code if GTA 6 developer Rockstar did not contact him promptly. Kurtaj's access to this supposed source code is unknown, but more recent GTA 6 leaks have raised questions about hackers still potentially having access to an apparently playable build of the game.

Beyond targeting a major release, Kurtaj gained notoriety due to his hacking methods and overt circumstances. The breach was carried out using an Amazon Fire Stick connected to a hotel room television while the teenager was on bail for another offense. The hacker had reportedly used a SIM-swapping scheme to gain access to bank accounts and other sensitive information before targeting Rockstar Games. Kurtaj is associated with a larger hacking group, Lapsus$, and is accused of causing £10 million ($13.19 million) in damages.

His recent court appearance detailed more antics, like an extortion attempt against Uber in which he "posted a picture of a penis as part of his attempts to force the company to pay a ransom." The BBC's report also states that Kurtaj's custody has been rocky: He is accused of physically assaulting and biting staff caring for him.

"His offending was calculated and, even after being charged with serious offences, he continued to target organisations, demonstrating a persistent disregard for the harm he was causing and the law," wrote Robert Hutchinson, Specialist Prosecutor for the Crown Prosecution Service, in a press release.

Kurtaj will be sentenced on Nov. 23, four days after the official launch of Grand Theft Auto 6.

FBI says Artex AI open-source hacking tool used to breach networks in ‘under three hours’

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The FBI is warning that hackers are relying on autonomous AI hacking tools to launch cyberattacks across the U.S. and globally. The campaigns include a widespread hack of customers of American IT company ConnectWise and across South Korea’s financial industry.

In its latest flash cybersecurity advisory, the FBI says hackers have targeted several industries in recent months, including financial services, government, tech, defense, phone and internet companies, and law firms, using open-source tools like Artex to carry out autonomous-driven cyberattacks.

Artex is one of several open-source AI-powered penetration testing platforms, a category of software built to probe a network’s defenses. Hackers can connect Artex to hosted frontier large language models (LLMs) like those from Anthropic and OpenAI, as well as to open-source models and non-U.S. models. The FBI says Artex intrusions can be “noisy,” meaning they generate reams of traffic and data that may trigger a targeted company’s defensive alerts. But the tools allow hackers to move at speeds that makes it far more harder for human defenders to keep up.

In some cases, Artex-linked attacks can move from targeting a system to active compromise “in under three hours.”

The FBI’s warning comes as AI-powered hacks are escalating, particularly by finding easy-to-find security flaws and breaking into companies with relative ease.

Oftentimes, Artex will rely on rapidly scanning a victim’s network to look for signs of weakness, and then exploit simple security bugs, such as weak protections and exposed or stolen passwords to gain a foothold. From there, Artex allows the hacker to spread across the network for databases, mailboxes, and other sensitive information.

The FBI’s advisory noted that Artex has been used in several cyberattacks since the tool’s first public debut in July. Artex was used to hack a number of South Korean financial institutions, blamed in large part on weak cybersecurity defenses and lacking protections, such as multi-factor authentication. The attacks allowed hackers to steal the personal data of at least 68,000 people.

The FBI confirmed earlier findings from Microsoft that hackers also used Artex to break into IT management giant ConnectWise and gain access to the Microsoft 365 cloud environments of at least 2,250 of its customers.

The hackers, who Microsoft says are likely Chinese, used the tool to steal data from affected ConnectWise customers.

A spokesperson for ConnectWise did not respond to a request for comment about the FBI’s claim.

Cybersecurity company Barracuda also disclosed that it was breached as part of an Artex-linked attack by financially motivated criminals, in which the tool found an exposed credential belonging to a third-party service that Barracuda had not revoked when it ended work with the vendor. That access allowed the hackers to access stores of credentials used by Barracuda to collect data from its customers’ systems. Barracuda said it couldn’t rule out access, as its logs were not detailed enough, and chose to “assume the worst” and changed all of its customer credentials.

“Defenders who rely on spotting malicious activity and then responding to it will often arrive after the attacker has finished,” wrote Barracuda’s chief information security officer Arve Kjoelen.

Barracuda did not return a request for comment about how many customers are affected by the breach.

After Artex was used in cyberattacks, the tool’s developer made the software closed-source to prevent further attacks. But the FBI says the previous open-source version continues to circulate and remains “widely accessible” for use in cyberattacks.

It’s not only companies and targeted victims that have been compromised as a result of weakened security exploited by AI tools; the tools themselves have been exposed as well, potentially allowing outsiders to access powerful hacking capabilities. Last week, researchers found hundreds of publicly exposed Artex systems online, which had been placed on the internet without a password, likely by mistake or misconfiguration — including the Artex system that was used to target South Korea.

Nokia, Telia, Verda, Nscale and Skeleton team up for EU AI gigafactory bid

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The AI gigafactory would link sites in Oulu, Iru and Valmiera, with a Swedish site still to be chosen. The EU’s tender closes on 3 December for one lot and 4 February for the other.

Mikko Hautala, Nokia’s chief geopolitical and government relations officer and chairman of Nokia Defense.

Nokia, Nscale, Skeleton, Telia and Verda have formed a group to bid for one of the EU’s planned AI gigafactories. Called AI Gigafactory Northern, it would spread one facility across several Nordic and Baltic sites, the partners said in a joint release on Monday.

The sites named so far are in Oulu in Finland, Iru in Estonia and Valmiera in Latvia. A fourth site, in Sweden, has no location yet. The partners plan to run them as one federated AI gigafactory under European governance.

The consortium plans to bid in a tender run by the European High-Performance Computing Joint Undertaking. That EU body also runs Europe’s shared supercomputers, such as Finland’s LUMI AI supercomputer. The call opened on 30 July and will pick the groups that build and run the gigafactories. The EU and its member states will then buy computing time from them.

Each gigafactory must hold three to four times as many top AI processors as Europe’s most powerful AI factories, the call says. It must also show enough power capacity and meet standards on energy and water efficiency.

The deadline for the call’s second lot is 3 December. On 5 November, the EU body moved the first lot’s deadline to 4 February 2027. The delay gives member states time to commit funding.

The release ties the plan to InvestAI, through which the EU aims to raise €30bn for AI. That includes €20bn from private companies and €10bn from EU and national public money, the partners said.

Nokia will supply the network that links the sites. AI cloud firm Nscale and Finland’s Verda bring platforms for training and running models. Telia, with its subsidiary Telia Cygate, adds connectivity, infrastructure operations and cloud services.

Estonia’s Skeleton Technologies makes power systems that it says ease the strain data centres put on the electricity grid. Two research partners, VTT Technical Research Centre of Finland and Sweden’s KTH Royal Institute of Technology, also back the group.

“Europe’s AI ambitions depend on infrastructure it can trust, control and scale,” said Mikko Hautala, chairman of Nokia Defense.

The partners said the region’s low-carbon energy and cool climate cut the power needed for cooling. Spreading capacity across borders also means no single site or supplier carries the whole load, they said.

“Skeleton contributes the power systems that ensure AI infrastructure doesn’t waste energy, places less strain on the grid, and gets more compute power from every GPU,” said Taavi Madiberk, Skeleton’s chief executive and co-founder.

Ainstein Opens Kansas Production Facility to Meet Growing Global Demand for Advanced Radar Sensors

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New Lenexa facility adds domestic manufacturing capacity for 44,000 radars annually.

LENEXA, Kan., Nov. 16, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Ainstein, a global radar sensor and solutions company, opened its new manufacturing facility in Lenexa, Kansas, on Friday with a ribbon-cutting ceremony. The expansion marks a significant milestone in the company's growth strategy and brings new production capacity to Kansas, where Ainstein was founded in 2015.

Ainstein's new Lenexa facility adds domestic manufacturing capacity for 44,000 radars annually to meet growing demand.

As demand for radar sensing grows across autonomous vehicles, unmanned systems, and commercial applications, customers require higher capacity, shorter lead times, a more resilient supply chain, and the ability to scale. Ainstein built the Lenexa facility to meet that demand, adding capacity for 44,000 radar sensors a year as the first of many steps in its strategic investment in long-term production capabilities.

Ainstein develops advanced radar sensors for aerospace, specialty and commercial vehicle, professional sports, and emerging technology markets. With over 150,000 sensors deployed worldwide, its radars provide safety and autonomy functions for unmanned aircraft, construction vehicles, agricultural equipment, and commercial fleets.

"We started Ainstein in Lawrence eleven years ago with a small team of engineers and a conviction about what radar could do," said Dr. Zongbo Wang, Founder and CEO of Ainstein. "Today our radars support autonomy in the air and on the ground in over 40 countries. This facility is the next chapter in Ainstein's growth strategy. It allows us to continue expanding our production to meet rising global demand and the long-term commitments we have made to our customers, and we are proud to be doing that in Kansas, where it all started."

The new facility adds a high-tech sensor manufacturer to the state's aerospace and manufacturing base. It also strengthens domestic supply for critical systems at a time when unmanned aircraft programs increasingly require locally produced sensors at volume. The added U.S. capacity gives Ainstein more room to support customer program and fleet growth while reinforcing its commitment to high-volume manufacturing and investment in Kansas.

The ribbon-cutting ceremony on November 13 welcomed employees, board members, business leaders, community representatives, and partners to the new facility for remarks and tours.

For more information about Ainstein's radar technology, engineering capabilities, and partnership opportunities, visit https://ainstein.ai.

Founded in Lawrence, Kansas, in 2015, Ainstein is a global leader in millimeter-wave radar sensing solutions for aerospace, automotive, and industrial markets. Combining deep radar engineering expertise, application-specific development, and dedicated technical support, Ainstein delivers advanced sensing technologies that help OEMs, government contractors, and systems integrators improve safety, efficiency, and autonomy across their products and platforms. Backed by Doosan Bobcat, Ainstein is headquartered in Lawrence, Kansas, with a production facility in Lenexa, Kansas.
